for the subsequence
s[k]{,}\ldots{,}s[j]
(
\varepsilon
if
k>j
),
and
s[\mathsf{last}]
for the last element of
s
.
We write
\sigma\in s
if
s[k]=\sigma
for some
1\leq k\leq\left|{s}\right|
.
We denote
a<_{s}b
(
a\leq_{s}b
) if there exists
j<k
(
j\leq k
) such that
a=s[j]
and
b=s[k]
.
We use “
\cdot
” for the concatenation of sequences,
and lift this notation to sets of sequences pointwise
(
S_{1}\cdot S_{2}\triangleq\{{s_{1}\cdot s_{2}\;|\;s_{1}\in S_{1},s_{2}\in S_{2}}\}
).
We often identify symbols with sequences of length
1
or their singletons (e.g., in expressions like
\sigma\cdot S
).
The
restriction
of a sequence
s
w.r.t. a set
\Gamma
, denoted by
s|_{\Gamma}
,
is the longest subsequence of
s
that consists only of symbols in
\Gamma
.
This notation is extended pointwise to sets of sequences
(
S|_{\Gamma}\triangleq\{{s|_{\Gamma}\;|\;s\in S}\}
).
We write
s_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}s_{2}
when
s_{1}
is a subsequence of
s_{2}
,
and
s_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}s_{2}
when
s_{1}
is a prefix of
s_{2}
.
When
s_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}s_{2}
, we denote by
s_{2}/s_{1}
the suffix
s
of
s_{2}
such that
s_{2}=s_{1}\cdot s
.
Given a sequences
s_{1}
and a subsequence
s_{2}=s_{1}[i\mathbin{..}j]
we denote by
\mathtt{pref}_{s_{1}}({s_{2}})
the maximal prefix of
s_{1}
such that
\mathtt{pref}_{s_{1}}({s_{2}})\cdot s_{2}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}s_{1}
.
Note that
\mathtt{pref}_{s_{1}}({\varepsilon})=s_{1}
.

Relations.

Given two relations
S,R
, we write
S\mathbin{;}R
to denote their composition.
Given a set
A
, we write
[A]
to denote the identity relation on
A
.
We also write
[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]
as a short for
[\{{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}}\}]
.

B.2
Labeled Transition Systems and Forward Simulations

Labeled transition systems.

A
labeled transition system
(LTS, for short) is a tuple
A={\langle{Q,\Sigma,q_{0},T}\rangle}
,
where
Q
is a set of
states
,

\Sigma
is a (possibly infinite) alphabet (whose elements are called
transition labels
),

q_{0}\in Q
is an
initial state
,
and
T\subseteq Q\times\Sigma\times Q
is a set of
transitions
.
We denote by
A.{\mathtt{Q}}
,
A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
,
A.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}
, and
A.{\mathtt{T}}
the components of an LTS
A
.
Given a transition
t={\langle{q,\sigma,q^{\prime}}\rangle}\in A.{\mathtt{T}}
, we let

\mathsf{pre}(t)\triangleq q
,
\mathsf{label}(t)\triangleq\sigma
, and
\mathsf{post}(t)\triangleq q^{\prime}
.
We write
{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}_{A}}{}
for the relation

\{{{\langle{q,q^{\prime}}\rangle}\;|\;{\langle{q,\sigma,q^{\prime}}\rangle}\in A.{\mathtt{T}}}\}
,
and
\xrightarrow{}_{A}
for
\bigcup_{\sigma\in\Sigma}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}_{A}}{}
.
For a sequence
\rho\in A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{*}
,
we write
{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rho}}$}_{A}}{}
for the composition
{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\rho[1]}$}_{A}}{}\mathbin{;}\ldots\mathbin{;}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\rho[\mathsf{last}]}$}_{A}}{}
.
A sequence
\rho\in A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{*}
such that
A.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rho}}$}_{A}}{}q

for some
q\in A.{\mathtt{Q}}
is a
trace
of
A
(or an
A
-trace
).
We denote by
\mathsf{traces}({A})
the set of all traces of
A
.
A state
q\in A.{\mathtt{Q}}
is
reachable
in
A
if

A.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rho}}$}_{A}}{}q
for some
\rho\in\mathsf{traces}({A})
.
A transition label
\sigma\in A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is
enabled
in a state
q\in A.{\mathtt{Q}}

if
q{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}_{A}}{}q^{\prime}
for some
q^{\prime}\in A.{\mathtt{Q}}
.
A sequence
e\in A.{\mathtt{T}}^{*}
such that
e=\varepsilon
, or
\mathsf{pre}(e[1])=q_{0}
and

\mathsf{post}(e[i])=\mathsf{pre}(e[i+1])
for all
1\leq i<\left|{e}\right|

is an
execution
of
A
.
We denote by
\mathsf{E}({A})
the set of all executions of
A
.
An
execution fragment

s\in A.{\mathtt{T}}^{*}
is a sequence of transitions such that
s=\varepsilon
or
s=e[i\mathbin{..}j]
for some
i\leq j
and
e\in\mathsf{E}({A})
.
We also define the pre-state and post-state of an execution fragment
s\neq\varepsilon
by
\mathsf{pre}(s)=\mathsf{pre}(s[1])
and
\mathsf{post}(s)=\mathsf{post}(s[\mathsf{last}])
.
The trace induced by an execution fragment
s
of
A

is the trace
\mathsf{trace}({s})
given by
(\mathsf{trace}({s}))[i]=\mathsf{label}(s[i])
for every
1\leq i\leq\left|{s}\right|
.
Note that we only consider finite executions and traces.

Forward simulations.

Given two LTSs
A
and
{A}^{\#}
and
a set
\Gamma\subseteq A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cap{A}^{\#}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
of “observable” transition labels,
a relation
R\subseteq A.{\mathtt{Q}}\times{A}^{\#}.{\mathtt{Q}}
is a

\Gamma
-forward simulation
from
A
to
{A}^{\#}
if:

(i)

{\langle{A.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}},{A}^{\#}.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}}\rangle}\in R
; and

(ii)

If
q{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}_{A}}{}q^{\prime}
and
{\langle{q,{q}^{\#}}\rangle}\in R
,
then there exist
{{q}^{\#}}^{\prime}\in{A}^{\#}.{\mathtt{Q}}
and
\rho\in{A}^{\#}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{*}

such that
{q}^{\#}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{${\mathrel{\to}^{\rho}}$}_{{A}^{\#}}}{}{{q}^{\#}}^{\prime}
,

\rho|_{\Gamma}=\sigma|_{\Gamma}
,
and
{\langle{q^{\prime},{{q}^{\#}}^{\prime}}\rangle}\in R
.

We write
A\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}^{\Gamma}{A}^{\#}
when such
R
exists.

Definition 24

(Execution Recorders)
.

The
execution recorder
of an LTS
A
, denoted by
{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
, is the LTS given by:

•

{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}.{\mathtt{Q}}=\mathsf{E}({A})

•

{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}

•

{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}=\varepsilon

•

{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}.{\mathtt{T}}=\{{{\langle{e,\mathsf{label}(t),e\cdot t}\rangle}\;|\;t\in A.{\mathtt{T}}\land e\cdot t\in\mathsf{E}({A})}\}

The LTS
{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
maintains the execution generated so far in each of its states.
The execution instrumentation does not affect the traces of
{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
and behaves similarly to “ghost variables”.
Thus, a simulation between
A
and
{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
(and vice-versa) can be easily established:

Proposition 25
.

For every LTS
A
,
we have
A\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}^{A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
and
{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}^{A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}A
.

Proof.

The relation
R\triangleq\{{{\langle{A.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}},\varepsilon}\rangle}}\}\cup\{{{\langle{q,e}\rangle}\;|\;e\in\mathsf{E}({A})\land\mathsf{post}(e[\mathsf{last}])=q}\}

is an
A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
-forward simulation from
A
to
{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
,
and its inverse is an
A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
-forward simulation from
{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
to
A
.
∎

Forward simulations are equivalent to mappings between executions with certain useful properties.
Given two LTSs
A,{A}^{\#}
, we say that a mapping
\pi:\mathsf{E}({A})\to\mathsf{E}({{A}^{\#}})
:

•

is
prefix-preserving
if
\pi(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon
and
\pi(e_{1})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{2})
for every
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({A})
such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.

•

respects
a set
\Gamma\subseteq A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cap{A}^{\#}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
if
\mathsf{trace}({e})|_{\Gamma}=\mathsf{trace}({\pi(e)})|_{\Gamma}
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({A})
.

Lemma 26
.

A\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}^{\Gamma}{A}^{\#}
iff there exists a prefix preserving mapping
\pi:\mathsf{E}({A})\to\mathsf{E}({{A}^{\#}})

that respects
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Given a
\Gamma
-forward simulation
R
from
A
to
{A}^{\#}
,
we obtain that there exists a choice function

f_{R}:(A.{\mathtt{T}}\times{A}^{\#}.{\mathtt{Q}})\to({A}^{\#}.{\mathtt{T}})^{*}

such that
{\langle{\mathsf{pre}(t),{q}^{\#}}\rangle}\in R

implies that
\mathsf{trace}({f_{R}(t,{q}^{\#})})|_{\Gamma}=\mathsf{label}(t)|_{\Gamma}
and
if
f_{R}(t,{q}^{\#})=\varepsilon
, then
{\langle{\mathsf{post}(t),{{q}^{\#}}}\rangle}\in R
,
and otherwise

{q}^{\#}=\mathsf{pre}(f_{R}(t,{q}^{\#}))

and
{\langle{\mathsf{post}(t),\mathsf{post}(f_{R}(t,{q}^{\#}))}\rangle}\in R
.
Then, for
e=t_{1}\mathinner{\cdotp\mkern-2.0mu\cdotp\mkern-2.0mu\cdotp}t_{n}\in\mathsf{E}({A})
, we define
\pi(e)\triangleq f_{R}(t_{1},{q}^{\#}_{0})\cdot f_{R}(t_{2},{q}^{\#}_{1})\mathinner{\cdotp\mkern-2.0mu\cdotp\mkern-2.0mu\cdotp}f_{R}(t_{n},{q}^{\#}_{n-1})

where
{q}^{\#}_{0}{,}\ldots{,}{q}^{\#}_{n-1}
are defined by

{q}^{\#}_{0}\triangleq{A}^{\#}.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}
and

{q}^{\#}_{i+1}\triangleq{q}^{\#}_{i}
if
f_{R}(t_{i+1},{q}^{\#}_{i})=\varepsilon
and otherwise
{q}^{\#}_{i+1}\triangleq\mathsf{post}(f_{R}(t_{i+1},{q}^{\#}_{i}))
.
It is straightforward to verify that
\pi
satisfies the required properties.

For the converse, by
Prop.

25
it suffices to show
{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}^{\Gamma}{{{A}^{\#}}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
.
Indeed,
\pi
itself (taken as a relation, i.e.,
R\triangleq\{{{\langle{e,\pi(e)}\rangle}\;|\;e\in\mathsf{E}({A})}\}
)
is a
\Gamma
-forward simulation from
{{A}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
to
{{{A}^{\#}}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
.
∎

Synchronizing LTSs.

The
interface parallel composition
of two LTSs
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
,
denoted by
{A_{1}}\parallel{A_{2}}
, is the LTS given by:

•

{A_{1}}\parallel{A_{2}}.{\mathtt{Q}}=A_{1}.{\mathtt{Q}}\times A_{2}.{\mathtt{Q}}
.

•

{A_{1}}\parallel{A_{2}}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=A_{1}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cup A_{2}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.

•

{A_{1}}\parallel{A_{2}}.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}={\langle{A_{1}.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}},A_{2}.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}}\rangle}
.

•

{A_{1}}\parallel{A_{2}}.{\mathtt{T}}
is defined by:

\frac{\sigma\in A_{1}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\setminus A_{2}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\quad\quad q_{1}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}}{}q^{\prime}_{1}}{{\langle{q_{1},q_{2}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}}{}{\langle{q^{\prime}_{1},q_{2}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{internal 1}}\frac{\sigma\in A_{2}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\setminus A_{1}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\quad\quad q_{2}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}}{}q^{\prime}_{2}}{{\langle{q_{1},q_{2}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}}{}{\langle{q_{1},q^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{internal 2}}\frac{\sigma\in A_{1}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cap A_{2}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\quad\quad q_{1}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}}{}q^{\prime}_{1}\quad q_{2}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}}{}q^{\prime}_{2}}{{\langle{q_{1},q_{2}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\sigma}$}}{}{\langle{q^{\prime}_{1},q^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{interface}}

The labels in
A_{1}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cap A_{2}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
are called
synchronization labels
.
Given an execution fragment
s={\langle{{\langle{q_{1}^{1},q_{2}^{1}}\rangle},\sigma_{1},{\langle{q_{1}^{2},q_{2}^{2}}\rangle}}\rangle}{,}\ldots{,}{\langle{{\langle{q_{1}^{n},q_{2}^{n}}\rangle},\sigma_{n},{\langle{q_{1}^{n+1},q_{2}^{n+1}}\rangle}}\rangle}\in({A_{1}}\parallel{A_{2}}).{\mathtt{T}}^{*}
, we write
s|_{A_{j}}
for the induced execution fragment of
A_{j}
,
that is

{\langle{q_{j}^{i_{1}},\sigma_{i_{1}},q_{j}^{{i_{1}}+1}}\rangle}{,}\ldots{,}{\langle{q_{j}^{i_{n_{j}}},\sigma_{i_{n_{j}}},q_{j}^{{i_{n_{j}}}+1}}\rangle}

where
i_{1}{,}\ldots{,}i_{n_{j}}
is an enumeration of all indices
1\leq i<n
such that
\sigma_{i}\in A_{j}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.
This notation is lifted to sets in the obvious way.

Proposition 27
.

e\in\mathsf{E}({{A_{1}}\parallel{A_{2}}})
iff
e|_{A_{1}}\in\mathsf{E}({A_{1}})
and
e|_{A_{2}}\in\mathsf{E}({A_{2}})
.

Equality of induced executions of a composed LTS implies equality of traces restricted to the alphabet of said LTS:

Lemma 28
.

Let
E\subseteq\mathsf{E}({{A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})
and
{E}^{\#}\subseteq\mathsf{E}({{{A}^{\#}}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})
be sets of executions such that
E|_{A^{\prime}}={E}^{\#}|_{A^{\prime}}
.
Then
\mathsf{traces}({E})|_{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}=\mathsf{traces}({{E}^{\#}})|_{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
.

Proof.

Given
\rho\in\mathsf{traces}({E})|_{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
,
there exists an execution

e\in E
such that

\rho=\mathsf{trace}({e})|_{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
.
Since
\mathsf{trace}({e|_{A^{\prime}}})=\mathsf{trace}({e})|_{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
, we obtain that
\rho=\mathsf{trace}({e|_{A^{\prime}}})
.
As
e\in E
we obtain that

e|_{A^{\prime}}\in E|_{A^{\prime}}={E}^{\#}|_{A^{\prime}}
.
That is, there exists an execution
{e}^{\#}\in{E}^{\#}
such that

{{e}^{\#}}|_{A^{\prime}}=e|_{A^{\prime}}
and thus

\rho=\mathsf{trace}({e|_{A^{\prime}}})=\mathsf{trace}({{e}^{\#}|_{A^{\prime}}})
.
Since
\mathsf{trace}({{e}^{\#}|_{A^{\prime}}})=\mathsf{trace}({{e}^{\#}})|_{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
, we obtain that

\rho=\mathsf{trace}({{e}^{\#}})|_{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\in\mathsf{traces}({{E}^{\#}})|_{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
.
The converse inclusion is proven symmetrically,
and we obtain that
\mathsf{traces}({E})|_{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}=\mathsf{traces}({{E}^{\#}})|_{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
.
∎

In the following we show that a simulation
A\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}^{\Gamma}{A}^{\#}
induces a simulation
{A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}^{A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{{A}^{\#}}\parallel{A^{\prime}}

for every LTS
A^{\prime}
such that
A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cap A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cap{A}^{\#}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\Gamma
.
Similarly to
Lemma

26
, this in turn induces a mapping
\pi:\mathsf{E}({{A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})\to\mathsf{E}({{{A}^{\#}}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})
,
with several useful properties in addition to those guaranteed by
Lemma

26
:

Theorem 29
.

Suppose that
A\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}^{\Gamma}{A}^{\#}

and let
A^{\prime}
be an LTS such that
A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cap A.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cap{A}^{\#}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\Gamma
.
Then, there exists a prefix preserving mapping
\pi:\mathsf{E}({{A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})\to\mathsf{E}({{{A}^{\#}}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})

with the following additional properties:

•

e|_{A^{\prime}}=\pi(e)|_{A^{\prime}}
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({A})
.

•

\mathsf{label}(\pi(e)[\mathsf{last}])\in A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
or
\pi(e)=\varepsilon
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({{A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})
.

•

\pi(e\cdot t)=\pi(e)
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({{A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})
and
t\in({A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}).{\mathtt{T}}
such that
e\cdot t\in\mathsf{E}({{A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})
and
\mathsf{label}(t)\notin A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.

•

For every
e\in\mathsf{E}({{A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})
and
t\in({A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}).{\mathtt{T}}
such that
e\cdot t\in\mathsf{E}({{A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}})
and
\mathsf{label}(t)\in A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\setminus\Gamma
,
there exists
{t}^{\#}\in({{A}^{\#}}\parallel{A^{\prime}}).{\mathtt{T}}
such that
\mathsf{label}({t}^{\#})=\mathsf{label}(t)
and
\pi(e\cdot t)=\pi(e)\cdot{t}^{\#}
.

Proof.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-forward simulation from
A
to
{A}^{\#}
.
We define a relation
R^{\prime}
by:

R^{\prime}=\{{{\langle{{\langle{q,q^{\prime}}\rangle},{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle}}\rangle}\;|\;q^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}.{\mathtt{Q}}\land\exists{{q}^{\#}}^{\prime}\in{A}^{\#}.{\mathtt{Q}},{\rho}^{\#}\in({A}^{\#}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\setminus\Gamma)^{*}\ldotp{\langle{q,{{q}^{\#}}^{\prime}}\rangle}\in R\wedge{q}^{\#}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{${\mathrel{\to}^{{\rho}^{\#}}}$}_{{A}^{\#}}}{}{{q}^{\#}}^{\prime}}\}

It is straightforward to verify that
R^{\prime}
is an
A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
-forward simulation from
{A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}
to
{{A}^{\#}}\parallel{A^{\prime}}
.
Moreover, similarly to the proof of
Lemma

26
we can use
R^{\prime}
to obtain a choice function

f_{R^{\prime}}:(({A}\parallel{A^{\prime}}).{\mathtt{T}}\times({{A}^{\#}}\parallel{A^{\prime}}).{\mathtt{Q}})\to({{A}^{\#}}\parallel{A^{\prime}}).{\mathtt{T}}^{*}

with the following properties (stronger than those obtained when constructing a choice function from an arbitrary forward simulation):

•

{\langle{\mathsf{pre}(t),{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle}}\rangle}\in R^{\prime}

implies that
f_{R^{\prime}}(t,{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle})|_{A^{\prime}}=t|_{A^{\prime}}
and
if
f_{R^{\prime}}(t,{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle})=\varepsilon
, then
{\langle{\mathsf{post}(t),{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle}}\rangle}\in R^{\prime}
,
and otherwise

{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle}=\mathsf{pre}(f_{R^{\prime}}(t,{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle}))

and

{\langle{\mathsf{post}(t),\mathsf{post}(f_{R^{\prime}}(t,{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle}))}\rangle}\in R^{\prime}
.

•

(f_{R^{\prime}}(t,{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle})[\mathsf{last}])|_{A^{\prime}}=t|_{A^{\prime}}
if
\mathsf{label}(t)\in A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
and
f_{R^{\prime}}(t,{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle})=\varepsilon
otherwise.

•

f_{R^{\prime}}(t,{\langle{{q}^{\#},q^{\prime}}\rangle})|_{A^{\prime}}=t|_{A^{\prime}}
if
\mathsf{label}(t)\in A^{\prime}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\setminus\Gamma
.

Then, we proceed to construct a mapping
\pi
from
f_{R^{\prime}}
as done in the proof of
Lemma

26
.
It is straightforward to verify
\pi
has all required properties.
∎

B.3
Objects and Programs

We assume a set
\mathsf{Tid}
of thread identifiers and an infinite set
\mathsf{Id}
of action identifiers.

Objects.

An
object
is a pair
O={\langle{\mathsf{M},\mathsf{Val}}\rangle}
, where

\mathsf{M}
is a set of method names and

\mathsf{Val}
is a set of values.
An object
O
is associated with actions divided to
invocations

i=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathit{m},v,p,k}\rangle}\in\mathsf{I}(O)

and
responses

r=\mathtt{res}{\langle{\mathit{m},v,p,k}\rangle}\in\mathsf{R}(O)
,
where
\mathit{m}\in\mathsf{M}
,
v\in\mathsf{Val}\cup\{{\bot}\}
,
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
, and
k\in\mathsf{Id}
.
We use the functions
{\mathtt{method}}
,
{\mathtt{val}}
,
{\mathtt{tid}}
, and
{\mathtt{id}}
,
to retrieve the method name (
\mathit{m}
),
value (
v
),
thread identifier (
p
),
and identifier (
k
)
of a given action
a\in\mathsf{Act}
.
We use
\bot
as argument/return value for methods without input/output.
We let
\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)\triangleq\mathsf{I}(O)\cup\mathsf{R}(O)
.
We sometimes lift the notation defined for actions to LTS transitions whose labels are these actions, e.g.
{\mathtt{id}}(t)={\mathtt{id}}(\mathsf{label}(t))
.

Histories.

A
history

h
of an object
O
is a finite sequence of invocations and responses of
O
.
A history
h
is
sequential
if it is alternating between invocations and responses (starting with an invocation and ending either with an invocation or a response),
such that every consecutive
i
,
r
in
h
has the same method and thread identifiers,
and a unique action identifier across
h
.
A history
h
is
well-formed
if for every
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
, the restriction of
h
to actions of
p
, denoted by
h|_{p}
, is sequential.
An invocation
i\in h
is
pending
if there is no response in
h
with the same thread and action identifiers.
Otherwise,
i
is
complete
.
We use
h|_{\mathit{m}}
for the restriction of
h
to actions with method
\mathit{m}
.

Operations.

We apply the above notions also on
operations

o
,
which are either single invocations
o=i
(called a
pending
operation) or pairs of matching invocation and response
o={\langle{i,r}\rangle}
(called a
completed
operation).
We let
\mathsf{completed}(h)
denote the subsequence of
h
consisting of actions that are a part of completed operations.
The functions
{\mathtt{method}}
,
{\mathtt{tid}}
,
{\mathtt{id}}
are lifted to operations by defining:

{\mathtt{method}}(o)\triangleq{\mathtt{method}}(o[1])
,
{\mathtt{tid}}(o)\triangleq{\mathtt{tid}}(o[1])
, and
{\mathtt{id}}(o)\triangleq{\mathtt{id}}(o[1])
.
An
operation identifier
is a pair in
\mathsf{OPid}\triangleq\mathsf{Tid}\times\mathsf{Id}
.
The operation identifier of an operation
o
is given by
{\mathtt{opid}}(o)\triangleq{\langle{{\mathtt{tid}}(o),{\mathtt{id}}(o)}\rangle}
.
An
operation’s input
is defined by
{\mathtt{input}}(o)\triangleq{\mathtt{val}}(o[1])
,
and an
operation’s output
is defined by
{\mathtt{output}}(o)\triangleq{\mathtt{val}}(o[\mathsf{last}])
(when
o
is completed).
Given an operation
o
and a history
h
, we sometimes write
o\in h
instead of
o\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}h
.
We let
\mathsf{set}(h)
denote the set of all operations that occur in
h
.
Given a method name
\mathit{m}\in\mathsf{M}
, we write
[\mathit{m}]
as a short for
[\{{o\;|\;{\mathtt{method}}(o)=\mathit{m}}\}]
- the identity relation over operations of method
\mathit{m}
.
Given two sequential histories
s_{1},s_{2}
we define their disjoint concatenation
s_{1}\oplus s_{2}
as the sequence with
s_{1}
followed by the subsequence of
s_{2}
composed of operations not present in
s_{1}
. Formally,
s_{1}\oplus s_{2}=s_{1}\cdot(s_{2}|_{\mathsf{set}(s_{2})\setminus\mathsf{set}(s_{1})})
.

Real-time Order.

The
real time order
induced by
a well-formed history
h
, denoted by
<_{h}
,
is the partial order on operations defined by
o_{1}<_{h}o_{2}
iff
the response of
o_{1}
appears in
h
before the invocation of
o_{2}
.
The notation
o_{1}<_{h}o_{2}
is also used when
o_{2}
is a completed operation but only its invocation appears in
h
.
We also define
o_{1}\leq_{h}o_{2}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{\triangle}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}}o_{1}<_{h}o_{2}\lor o_{1}=o_{2}

Specifications.

A
specification
of an object
O
is a prefix closed set of sequential histories of
O
.

Object Implementations.

We assume a set
\mathsf{IInt}
of labels for implementation internal actions and define an

implementation

I
of an object
O
to be an LTS over the alphabet
\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)\cup\mathsf{IInt}
.
For all implementations presented in the paper we will use

\mathsf{IInt}=\{{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}\;|\;p\in\mathsf{Tid}}\}

We assume that the history induced by every execution
e
of
I
, denoted by
\mathsf{h}({e})
, is a well-formed history.
Intuitively, executions of
I
represent executions generated
by the methods’ implementations when they repeatedly and concurrently invoked with arbitrary arguments.

Client Programs.

We assume a set
\mathsf{PInt}
of labels for client internal actions (disjoint from
\mathsf{IInt}
) and define a
client program
P
for an object
O
as an LTS over the alphabet
P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.

Linking Client Programs and Object Implementations.

The
linking
of a program
P
and implementation
I
, denoted by
P[I]
,
is the interface parallel composition of
P
and
I
,
i.e.,
P[I]={I}\parallel{P}
.

B.4
Linearizability

Definition 30
.

A sequential history
s\in\mathit{Spec}
is a
linearization
of a history
h
, denoted
h\sqsubseteq s
,
if there exist a sequence of responses
\bar{r}\in\mathsf{R}^{*}
for pending invocations in
h
such that the following hold for
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{completed}(h\cdot\bar{r})
:

•

h^{\prime}|_{p}=s|_{p}
for every
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
.

•

<_{h^{\prime}}\;\subseteq\;<_{s}
.

A history
h
is called
linearizable
w.r.t.
\mathit{Spec}
if it has a linearization
s\in\mathit{Spec}
.

The first condition on
h^{\prime}
and
s
can be simplified, as follows:

Lemma 31
.

h\sqsubseteq s
iff there exists a sequence
\bar{r}\in\mathsf{R}^{*}
of responses for pending invocations in
h
such that the following hold for
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{completed}(h\cdot\bar{r})
:

•

\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})=\mathsf{set}(s)
.

•

<_{h^{\prime}}\;\subseteq\;<_{s}
.

Proof.

In one direction, since every operation belongs to some thread, we have that
\forall p\in\mathsf{Tid}\ldotp h^{\prime}|_{p}=s|_{p}\implies\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})=\mathsf{set}(s)
.
For the converse, we have
\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})=\mathsf{set}(s)\implies\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime}|_{p})=\mathsf{set}(s|_{p})
for every
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
.
As
h^{\prime}|_{p}
is sequential, we obtain that both
<_{h^{\prime}|_{p}},<_{s|_{p}}
are total orders over the same set of operations.
Using
<_{h^{\prime}}\;\subseteq\;<_{s}
we get that
<_{h^{\prime}|_{p}}\;\subseteq\;<_{s|_{p}}
and so overall
h^{\prime}|_{p}=s|_{p}
.
∎

Definition 32
.

Let
I
be an implementation and
\mathit{Spec}
a specification.
A function
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}
is a
linearization mapping

if
\mathsf{h}({e})\sqsubseteq L(e)
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
.
We say
I
is
linearizable
w.r.t.
\mathit{Spec}

if there exists a linearization mapping
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}
.

Next, we prove several lemmas that simplify the construction of linearization mappings from existing ones.
The first is used to show that a sequential history linearizes some history,
given that some other sequential history linearizes that history.

Lemma 33
.

If
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathit{Spec}
,
\mathsf{set}(s)=\mathsf{set}(s^{\prime})
, and for some history
h
we have
h\sqsubseteq s
and
o<_{h}o^{\prime}
implies
o<_{s^{\prime}}o^{\prime}
for all
o,o^{\prime}\in\mathsf{set}(s^{\prime})
,
then
h\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{r}
be the sequence of responses such that the conditions in
Lemma

31
hold for
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{completed}(h\cdot\bar{r})
and
s
.
That is,
\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})=\mathsf{set}(s)
and
<_{h^{\prime}}\subseteq<_{s}
.
We claim the same conditions hold for
h^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
.
Indeed,
by definition of linearizability,
\mathsf{set}(s)=\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})
, and as
\mathsf{set}(s)=\mathsf{set}(s^{\prime})
, we get that
\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})=\mathsf{set}(s^{\prime})
.
It remains to verify that
<_{h^{\prime}}\subseteq<_{s^{\prime}}
.
Indeed, assume
o<_{h^{\prime}}o^{\prime}
. Using the lemma’s assumptions it is sufficient to verify that
o,o^{\prime}\in\mathsf{set}(s^{\prime})
and
o<_{h}o^{\prime}
.
The former follows from
\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})=\mathsf{set}(s^{\prime})
, and the latter holds since real time order is not effected by the addition of a suffix of responses, which means
<_{h}=<_{h\cdot\bar{r}}\supseteq<_{h^{\prime}}
.
∎

The next lemma also enables generating a linearization from an existing linearization
by removing operations that are not completed in the execution inducing the linearized history.

Lemma 34
.

If
s,\hat{s}\in\mathit{Spec}
,
\hat{s}\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}s
,
h\sqsubseteq s
for some history
h
and
\mathsf{set}(\mathsf{completed}(h))\subseteq\mathsf{set}(\hat{s})
then
h\sqsubseteq\hat{s}
.

Proof.

From
h\sqsubseteq s
there exist a sequence of responses
\bar{r}\in\mathsf{R}^{*}
for pending invocations in
h
such that the conditions in
Lemma

31
hold for
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{completed}(h\cdot\bar{r})
and
s
.
That is,
\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})=\mathsf{set}(s)
and
<_{h^{\prime}}\subseteq<_{s}
.
For every
o\in\mathsf{set}(s)\setminus\mathsf{set}(\hat{s})
it must be the case that
o\notin\mathsf{set}(\mathsf{completed}(h))
and so for
i\in\mathsf{I}
,
r\in\mathsf{R}
the inv-res pair such that
o=i\cdot r

we have
i\in h
,
r\in\bar{r}
.
If we take
\bar{r}^{\prime}\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}\bar{r}
to be the subsequence with each such
r
removed, then for
h^{\prime\prime}\triangleq\mathsf{completed}(h\cdot\bar{r}^{\prime})

we have
\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})\setminus\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime\prime})=\mathsf{set}(s)\setminus\mathsf{set}(\hat{s})
.
As
\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})=\mathsf{set}(s)
, we can deduce that
\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime\prime})=\mathsf{set}(\hat{s})
.
Moreover,
<_{h^{\prime\prime}}=<

the same operations are removed from
h^{\prime\prime}\triangleq\mathsf{completed}(h\cdot\bar{r}^{\prime})
compared to
h^{\prime}
and from
\hat{s}
compared to
s
, which means the conditions in
Lemma

31
hold for
h^{\prime\prime}
and
\hat{s}
, and we get that
h\sqsubseteq\hat{s}
.
∎

Finally, if a sequential history linearizes the history of some execution, and we append a non-response transition,
then the same sequential history linearizes the extended execution:

Lemma 35
.

If
I
is an implementation,

e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
is an execution,

t\in I.{\mathtt{T}}
is a transition such that
e\cdot t\in\mathsf{E}({I})
and
t\notin\mathsf{R}
,

s\in\mathit{Spec}
,
and
\mathsf{h}({e})\sqsubseteq s
,
then
\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})\sqsubseteq s
.

Proof.

From
\mathsf{h}({e})\sqsubseteq s
,
there exists a sequence
\bar{r}\in\mathsf{R}^{*}
such that the conditions in
Definition

30
hold for
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e})\cdot\bar{r})
and
s
.
It is sufficient to show that
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})\cdot\bar{r})
, and thus
\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})\sqsubseteq s
follows using the same sequence
\bar{r}
.

Indeed, denoting
a=\mathsf{label}(t)
we have
a\in\mathsf{IInt}
or
a\in\mathsf{I}
.
If
a\in\mathsf{IInt}
then
\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})=\mathsf{h}({e})
and we are done.
Otherwise,
a\in\mathsf{I}
and we have
\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})=\mathsf{h}({e})\cdot a
.
We have that
a
does not have a matching response in
\bar{r}
since
\bar{r}
only contains responses for pending invocations in
\mathsf{h}({e})
.
Hence,

\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})\cdot\bar{r})=\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e})\cdot a\cdot\bar{r})=\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e})\cdot\bar{r})
.
∎

B.5
Registers

A register object is given by
\mathsf{Reg}={\langle{\{{\mathtt{read},\mathtt{write}}\},{\mathbb{N}}}\rangle}
.
Its specification, denoted by
\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
, assumes that
0
is the initial register value,
and is formally defined as follows.

Definition 36
.

The value observed after a sequential history
s
of
\mathsf{Reg}
,
denoted by
\mathtt{obs}(s)\in\mathsf{Val}
,
is defined as follows:

\mathtt{obs}(s)\triangleq\begin{cases}0&s|_{\mathtt{write}}=\varepsilon\\
{\mathtt{input}}(\mathsf{last_{op}}(s|_{\mathtt{write}}))&s|_{\mathtt{write}}\neq\varepsilon\\
\end{cases}

Definition 37
.

The set
\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
consists of all sequential histories
s
such that for all operations
o\in s
:

•

If
{\mathtt{method}}(o)=\mathtt{read}
, then
{\mathtt{input}}(o)=\bot
and
{\mathtt{output}}(o)=\mathtt{obs}(\mathtt{pref}_{s}({o}))
.

•

If
{\mathtt{method}}(o)=\mathtt{write}
, then
{\mathtt{input}}(o)\in\mathsf{Val}
and
{\mathtt{output}}(o)=\bot
.

Note that
s\cdot\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{read},\bot,p,k}\rangle}\cdot\mathtt{res}{\langle{\mathtt{read},\mathtt{obs}(s),p,k}\rangle}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
for any operation identifier
{\langle{p,k}\rangle}\in\mathsf{OPid}
that is not the identifier of some operation in
s
.

The following lemmas are useful for constructing register linearizations from existing linearizations.
First, register sequential histories remain legal when removing read operations from them.

Lemma 38
.

If
s\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
, then
\hat{s}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
for every sequential history
\hat{s}
such that
s|_{\mathtt{write}}\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}\hat{s}\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}s
.

Proof.

Follows from the fact that
\mathtt{obs}
only depends on write operations and the sequence of write operations before every read is the same in
\hat{s}
and
s
.
∎

The next lemma is used to show that a sequence is a valid register sequential history, using other valid histories.
This is done by showing each read comes after a sequence of writes which it also comes after in some other valid sequential history.

Lemma 39
.

If
s
is a sequential history of
\mathsf{Reg}
such that
\mathsf{set}(s)\subseteq\mathsf{set}(s^{\prime})
for some
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
and for each read operation
r\in s|_{\mathtt{read}}
we have
\mathtt{pref}_{s}({r})|_{\mathtt{write}}=\mathtt{pref}_{s_{r}}({r})|_{\mathtt{write}}
for some
s_{r}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
such that
r\in\mathsf{set}(s_{r})
,
then
s\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
.

Proof.

First, the condition
\mathsf{set}(s)\subseteq\mathsf{set}(s^{\prime})
and
s^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
ensure that the inputs of reads and outputs of writes are
\bot
, and inputs of writes are in
\mathsf{Val}
.

Moreover, for each read operation
r\in s|_{\mathtt{read}}
we have
\mathtt{pref}_{s}({r})|_{\mathtt{write}}=\mathtt{pref}_{s_{r}}({r})|_{\mathtt{write}}
which implies

\mathtt{obs}(\mathtt{pref}_{s}({r}))=\mathtt{obs}(\mathtt{pref}_{s_{r}}({r}))
.
As
s_{r}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
we have that
{\mathtt{output}}(r)=\mathtt{obs}(\mathtt{pref}_{s_{r}}({r}))
and thus overall
{\mathtt{output}}(r)=\mathtt{obs}(\mathtt{pref}_{s}({r}))
as required.
∎

B.6
Hyperproperties, schedulers and strong observational refinement

Schedulers.

Given a program
P
and an implementation
I
,
a
scheduler
is a function
S:\mathsf{E}({P[I]})\to\mathcal{P}({P[I].{\mathtt{T}}})
.
An execution
e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I]})
is
consistent with
S
if

e[j]\in S(e[1]\mathinner{\cdotp\mkern-2.0mu\cdotp\mkern-2.0mu\cdotp}e[j-1])
for every
1\leq j\leq\left|{e}\right|
.
We denote by
\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})
the set of executions of
P[I]

that are consistent with
S
,
and by
\mathsf{traces}({P[I],S})
the traces of executions in
\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})
.
A scheduler is
deterministic
if for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I]})
,
either
\left|{S(e)}\right|\leq 1
or all transitions in
S(e)
are labeled by actions in
\mathsf{PInt}
.

Deterministic schedulers have the following useful property:
if two executions generated by a deterministic scheduler induce the same program execution,
then one of the executions must be a prefix of the other:

Lemma 40
.

Let
I
be an implementation of an object
O
and
P
a client program for
O
,
and let
S:\mathsf{E}({P[I]})\to\mathcal{P}({P[I].{\mathtt{T}}})
be a deterministic scheduler.
Then
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
or
e_{2}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{1}

for every
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})

such that
e_{1}|_{P}=e_{2}|_{P}
.

Proof.

Assume
e_{1},e_{2}
are not prefixes of each other.
Let
j
be the minimal index such that
e_{1}[j]\neq e_{2}[j]
.
Then
e_{1}[j],e_{2}[j]\in S(e_{1}[1\mathbin{..}j-1])

and thus
\left|{S(e_{1}[1\mathbin{..}j-1])}\right|>1
.
From
S
being deterministic,
it must be the case that
\mathsf{label}(e_{1}[j]),\mathsf{label}(e_{2}[j])\in\mathsf{PInt}
,
contradicting
e_{1}|_{P}=e_{2}|_{P}
.
∎

Next, we show that
the mapping of executions generated by a deterministic scheduler to their induced program executions is a bijection
when considering only executions ending in a program transition (i.e., an invocation, a response, or a program internal transition).
Moreover, both the mapping and its inverse are prefix preserving:

Lemma 41
.

Let
I
be an implementation of an object
O
and
P
a client program for
O
,
and let
S:\mathsf{E}({P[I]})\to\mathcal{P}({P[I].{\mathtt{T}}})
be a deterministic scheduler.
Then the function

p:(\{{\varepsilon}\}\cup\{{e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\;|\;\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\})\to\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})|_{P}

defined by
p(e)=e|_{P}
is a bijection
and both
p
and
p^{-1}
are prefix preserving.

Proof.

For injectivity, let
e_{1},e_{2}\in\{{\varepsilon}\}\cup\{{e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\;|\;\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}

be two executions such that
e_{1}|_{P}=e_{2}|_{P}
.
By
Lemma

40
we obtain w.l.o.g., that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.
Assume for contradiction that
e_{1}\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.
Then
e_{2}\neq\varepsilon
and in particular
e_{2}[\mathsf{last}]
is a program transition (i.e.,
\mathsf{label}(e_{2}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
).
We deduce that
e_{1}|_{P}\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}|_{P}
,
a contradiction.
Therefore,
e_{1}=e_{2}
.

For surjectivity, let
e_{p}\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})|_{P}
.
Then by definition there exists some execution
e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})
such that
e_{p}=e|_{P}
.
Let
e^{\prime}
be the maximal prefix of
e
such that
\mathsf{label}(e^{\prime}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, or
e^{\prime}=\varepsilon
if no such prefix exists.
Then we get that
p(e^{\prime})=e^{\prime}|_{P}=e|_{P}=e_{p}
.

The fact that
p
is prefix preserving is immediate.
To show that
p^{-1}
is also prefix preserving, due to bijectivity of
p
it suffices to prove:

•

p(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon
:

Indeed
p(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon|_{P}=\varepsilon
.

•

p(e_{1})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}p(e_{2})\implies e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\{{\varepsilon}\}\cup\{{e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\;|\;\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}
:

Indeed,
p(e_{1})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}p(e_{2})
means
e_{1}|_{P}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}|_{P}
.
Taking
e^{\prime}_{2}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
the minimal prefix of
e_{2}
such that
e_{1}|_{P}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e^{\prime}_{2}|_{P}
, we obtain that
e_{1}|_{P}=e^{\prime}_{2}|_{P}
and either
e^{\prime}_{2}=\varepsilon
or
\mathsf{label}(e^{\prime}_{2}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.

e^{\prime}_{2}
is consistent with
S
since
e_{2}
is consistent with
S
, and so
e^{\prime}_{2}
is in the domain of
p
and
p(e^{\prime}_{2})=e^{\prime}_{2}|_{P}
. Thus, from
e_{1}|_{P}=e^{\prime}_{2}|_{P}
, we deduce that
p(e_{1})=p(e^{\prime}_{2})
. Combined with the injectivity of
p
proven above we get that
e_{1}=e^{\prime}_{2}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
as required.∎

Hyperproperties.

A
hyperproperty

\phi
of a program
P
is a set of sets of the program’s traces
(i.e.,
\phi\subseteq\mathcal{P}({\mathsf{traces}({P})})
).
An implementation
I

satisfies a hyperproperty

\phi
of
P
,
denoted by
I\models_{P}\phi
,
if
\mathsf{traces}({P[I],S})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\in\phi
for every deterministic scheduler
S
.

Strong Observational Refinement.

An implementation
I

strongly observationally refines
an implementation
{I}^{\#}
,
denoted by
I\leq_{\mathsf{s}}{I}^{\#}
,
if
{I}^{\#}\models_{P}\phi\implies I\models_{P}\phi

for every program
P
and hyperproperty
\phi
of
P
.
The following alternative characterization follows from the definition.

Lemma 42
.

I\leq_{\mathsf{s}}{I}^{\#}
iff for every program
P
and deterministic scheduler
S
,
there exists a deterministic scheduler
{S}^{\#}
such that

\mathsf{traces}({P[I],S})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}=\mathsf{traces}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
.

Proof.

Assume
I\leq_{\mathsf{s}}{I}^{\#}
.
Let
P
be a program and consider the hyperproperty

\phi=\{{\mathsf{traces}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\;|\;{S}^{\#}\text{ is a deterministic scheduler}}\}

It is straightforward to verify that
\phi\subseteq\mathcal{P}({\mathsf{traces}({P})})
and
{I}^{\#}\models_{P}\phi
.
Therefore,
I\models_{P}\phi
.
This implies that for every deterministic scheduler
S
,

\mathsf{traces}({P[I],S})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\in\phi
and thus there exists a deterministic scheduler
{S}^{\#}
such that
\mathsf{traces}({P[I],S})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}=\mathsf{traces}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
.

The converse is immediate from the definitions.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of
Thm.

5
(
I\leq_{\mathsf{s}}{I}^{\#}\iff I\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}{I}^{\#}
)

In this appendix we prove
Thm.

5
.
A similar theorem is proved in
[
3
, Theorem 8]

for slightly different definitions of linearization mappings and schedulers, operating on traces instead of executions,
and for implementations that are step-deterministic LTSs.
For the sake of completeness, we reprove the result for our setting,
adapting in some cases (and somewhat simplifying in others) the proofs from
[
3
]
.

First, we prove that forward simulation is sufficient for strong observational refinement.

Theorem 43
.

Let
I,{I}^{\#}
be implementations of an object
O
.
If
I\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}{I}^{\#}
, then
I\leq_{\mathsf{s}}{I}^{\#}
.

Proof.

Let
P
be a client program and
S:\mathsf{E}({P[I]})\to\mathcal{P}({P[I].{\mathtt{T}}})
be a deterministic scheduler.
From
Thm.

29
, there exists a prefix preserving mapping
\pi:\mathsf{E}({P[I]})\to\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}]})
such that:

•

(respects
P
)
e|_{P}=\pi(e)|_{P}
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({P})
.

•

(
P
-ending)
\mathsf{label}(\pi(e)[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
or
\pi(e)=\varepsilon
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I]})
.

•

(
P
-activated)
\pi(e\cdot t)=\pi(e)
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I]})
and
t\in P[I].{\mathtt{T}}
such that
e\cdot t\in\mathsf{E}({P[I]})
and
\mathsf{label}(t)\notin P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.

•

(
\mathsf{PInt}
-copying)
For every
e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I]})
and
t\in P[I].{\mathtt{T}}
such that
e\cdot t\in\mathsf{E}({P[I]})
and
\mathsf{label}(t)\in\mathsf{PInt}
,
there exists
{t}^{\#}\in P[{I}^{\#}].{\mathtt{T}}
such that
{t}^{\#}|_{P}=t|_{P}
and
\pi(e\cdot t)=\pi(e)\cdot{t}^{\#}
.

We define a scheduler
{S}^{\#}:\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}]})\to\mathcal{P}({P[{I}^{\#}].{\mathtt{T}}})
as follows:

{S}^{\#}({e}^{\#})\triangleq\{{{t}^{\#}\in{A}^{\#}.{\mathtt{T}}\;|\;\exists e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\ldotp{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e)}\}

The scheduler
{S}^{\#}
allows transitions when they extend the current execution towards some execution obtained by applying
\pi

on some execution allowed by
S
.

The following claim states the main useful property of
{S}^{\#}
:

Claim 43.1:

For all
{t}^{\#}\in{S}^{\#}({e}^{\#})
, there exists an execution
e=e^{\prime}\cdot t\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\setminus\{{\varepsilon}\}
such that the following hold:

•

{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e)

•

\mathsf{label}(t)\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.

•

{e}^{\#}|_{P}=e^{\prime}|_{P}
.

•

If
\mathsf{label}({t}^{\#})\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, then
{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}=\pi(e)
.

{claimproof}

By definition of
{S}^{\#}
there exists an execution
e
satisfying
{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e)
.
Let
e_{\mathsf{m}}
be the minimal execution (w.r.t.
\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}
) satisfying
{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{\mathsf{m}})
.
Since
\pi
is
P
-activated,
it must be the case that
\mathsf{label}(e_{\mathsf{m}}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.
Denote
e_{\mathsf{m}}=e^{\prime}\cdot t
.
Let
e^{\prime\prime}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e^{\prime}
be the largest prefix of
e^{\prime}
(w.r.t.
\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}
) such that
\mathsf{label}(e^{\prime\prime}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.
Then from
\pi
being
P
-activated we have
\pi(e^{\prime})=\pi(e^{\prime\prime})
and from minimality of
e_{\mathsf{m}}
we have that

\pi(e^{\prime\prime})\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{\mathsf{m}})
.
Removing the transition
{t}^{\#}
we obtain that
\pi(e^{\prime})=\pi(e^{\prime\prime})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}{e}^{\#}\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{\mathsf{m}})
.
As
\mathsf{label}(\pi(e_{\mathsf{m}})[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
,
restricting to program transitions we deduce

\pi(e^{\prime})|_{P}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}{e}^{\#}|_{P}\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{\mathsf{m}})|_{P}
.
Using the fact that
\pi
respects
P
, we obtain:

e^{\prime}|_{P}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}{e}^{\#}|_{P}\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{\mathsf{m}}|_{P}
.
As
e_{\mathsf{m}}|_{P}=e^{\prime}|_{P}\cdot t
and
\mathsf{label}(t)\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}

we get overall that
{e}^{\#}|_{P}=e^{\prime}|_{P}
.

Assume now additionally that
{t}^{\#}\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
and
assume for contradiction
{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}\neq\pi(e)
.
Returning to the inequality

\pi(e^{\prime})\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{\mathsf{m}})

we deduce that

\pi(e^{\prime})\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{\mathsf{m}})
.
All executions here end in a program transitions and thus
\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}
is maintained when restricting to these transitions and similarly to above we can deduce

e^{\prime}|_{P}\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}{e}^{\#}|_{P}\cdot{t}^{\#}\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{\mathsf{m}}|_{P}
,
contradicting the fact that
e_{\mathsf{m}}|_{P}=e^{\prime}|_{P}\cdot t
.

When restricting
\pi
’s domain and codomain to executions ending in program transitions,
\pi
is a bijection:

Claim 43.2:

\pi
is a bijection from
\{{\varepsilon}\}\cup\{{e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\;|\;\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}
to
\{{\varepsilon}\}\cup\{{{e}^{\#}\in\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})\;|\;\mathsf{label}({e}^{\#}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}
.

{claimproof}

As
\pi(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon
,
it suffices to show
\pi
is a bijection from
\{{e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\;|\;\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}
to
\{{{e}^{\#}\in\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})\;|\;\mathsf{label}({e}^{\#}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}
.
Indeed, we have:

•

The codomain is valid:

Let
e\in\{{e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\;|\;\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}
.
Since
\pi
is
(P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
-ending,

\mathsf{label}(\pi(e)[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
,
and it remains to show
\pi(e)
is consistent with
{S}^{\#}
.
Indeed, let
1\leq j\leq\left|{\pi(e)}\right|
.
We have
\pi(e)[1\mathbin{..}j-1]\cdot\pi(e)[j]\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e)
,
and so
\pi(e)[j]\in{S}^{\#}(\pi(e)[1\mathbin{..}j-1])
.

•

\pi
is injective:

Let
e_{1},e_{2}\in\{{e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\;|\;\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}

be two traces such that
\pi(e_{1})=\pi(e_{2})
.
Then
\pi(e_{1})|_{P}=\pi(e_{2})|_{P}
.
Since
\pi
respects
P

we have that
e_{j}|_{P}=\pi(e_{j})|_{P}
.
Overall we obtain that
p(e_{1})=e_{1}|_{P}=\pi(e_{1})|_{P}=\pi(e_{2})|_{P}=e_{2}|_{P}=p(e_{2})
,
and thus
e_{1}=e_{2}
follows from the injectivity of
p
, proven in
Lemma

41
.

•

\pi
is surjective:

Let
{e}^{\#}\in\{{{e}^{\#}\in\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})\;|\;\mathsf{label}({e}^{\#}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}
.
Then
{e}^{\#}[\mathsf{last}]\in{S}^{\#}({e}^{\#}[1\mathbin{..}\left|{{e}^{\#}}\right|-1])
,
and applying
Appendix

C
(using
\mathsf{label}({e}^{\#}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
) we obtain an execution
e\in\{{e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\;|\;\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}
such that
\pi(e)={e}^{\#}
.∎

Next, we show
\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})|_{P}=\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P}
.
This is done by establishing a bijection
f:\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})|_{P}\to\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P}
,
and then showing it is the identity function.

For this purpose, using
Lemma

41
we have that the functions

\displaystyle p:

\displaystyle\{{\varepsilon}\}\cup\{{e\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\;|\;\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}\to\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})|_{P}

\displaystyle{p}^{\#}:

\displaystyle\{{\varepsilon}\}\cup\{{{e}^{\#}\in\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})\;|\;\mathsf{label}({e}^{\#}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}\to\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P}

defined by
p(e)=e|_{P}
and
{p}^{\#}({e}^{\#})={e}^{\#}|_{P}

are bijections.
Applying
C
we obtain that the function

f:\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})|_{P}\to\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P}

defined by
f(e_{p})={p}^{\#}(\pi(p^{-1}(e_{p})))

is a bijection.
Moreover, using the fact that
\pi
respects
P
we obtain for every
e_{p}\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})|_{P}
:

{f(e_{p})}={{p}^{\#}(\pi(p^{-1}(e_{p})))}={\pi(p^{-1}(e_{p}))|_{P}}={p^{-1}(e_{p})|_{P}}={p(p^{-1}(e_{p}))}={e_{p}}

We deduce
\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})|_{P}=\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P}
.
Applying
Lemma

28
this implies
\mathsf{traces}({P[I],S})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}=\mathsf{traces}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
.

Now, we show that
{S}^{\#}
is deterministic.
It suffices to prove that
for every
{t}^{\#}_{1},{t}^{\#}_{2}\in{S}^{\#}({e}^{\#})
,
either
{t}^{\#}_{1}={t}^{\#}_{2}
or
\mathsf{label}({t}^{\#}_{1}),\mathsf{label}({t}^{\#}_{2})\in\mathsf{PInt}
.

Applying
Appendix

C
to both
{t}^{\#}_{1}
and
{t}^{\#}_{2}
we obtain executions
e_{1}=e_{1}^{\prime}\cdot t_{1}
,
e_{2}=e_{2}^{\prime}\cdot t_{2}
such that

e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})\setminus\{{\varepsilon}\}
,

{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{1})
,
{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}_{2}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{2})
,
\mathsf{label}(t_{1}),\mathsf{label}(t_{2})\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
,
and
{e}^{\#}|_{P}=e^{\prime}_{1}|_{P}=e^{\prime}_{2}|_{P}
.
We show that
e^{\prime}_{1}=e^{\prime}_{2}
.
From
e^{\prime}_{1}|_{P}=e^{\prime}_{2}|_{P}
we obtain
using
Lemma

40
that w.l.o.g.,
e^{\prime}_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e^{\prime}_{2}
.
Assume for contradiction
e^{\prime}_{1}\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}e^{\prime}_{2}
, and let
t^{\prime}_{2}=e^{\prime}_{2}[\left|{e^{\prime}_{1}}\right|+1]
.
From
e^{\prime}_{1}|_{P}=e^{\prime}_{2}|_{P}
we get that
\mathsf{label}(t^{\prime}_{2})\notin P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
,
and thus
t_{1}\neq t^{\prime}_{2}
.
Therefore from
t_{1},t^{\prime}_{2}\in S(e^{\prime}_{1})
and from determinism of
S

we get that
\mathsf{label}(t^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathsf{PInt}
, a contradiction.
Hence it must be the case that
e^{\prime}_{1}=e^{\prime}_{2}
. Therefore,
t_{1},t_{2}\in S(e^{\prime}_{1})=S(e^{\prime}_{2})
.
From determinism of
S
,
this means either
t_{1}=t_{2}
or
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathsf{PInt}
.
In the first case, we obtain that
e_{1}=e_{2}
and from
{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{1})
and
{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}_{2}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{2})

we get that
{t}^{\#}_{1}={t}^{\#}_{2}
.
In the second case, from
\pi
being
\mathsf{PInt}
-copying we obtain that

\pi(e_{1})=\pi(e^{\prime}_{1})\cdot{{t}^{\#}_{1}}^{\prime}
and
\pi(e_{2})=\pi(e^{\prime}_{2})\cdot{{t}^{\#}_{2}}^{\prime}
for transitions
{{t}^{\#}_{1}}^{\prime},{{t}^{\#}_{2}}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{label}({{t}^{\#}_{1}}^{\prime})=\mathsf{label}(t_{1})
and
\mathsf{label}({{t}^{\#}_{2}}^{\prime})=\mathsf{label}(t_{2})
.
As
e^{\prime}_{1}=e^{\prime}_{2}
, the executions
\pi(e_{1})
and
\pi(e_{2})
only differ in the last transition.
This is only possible if either
{t}^{\#}_{1}={t}^{\#}_{2}
,
or
{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}_{1}=\pi(e_{1})
and
{e}^{\#}\cdot{t}^{\#}_{2}=\pi(e_{2})
.
The latter option implies
{t}^{\#}_{1}={{t}^{\#}_{1}}^{\prime}
and
{t}^{\#}_{2}={{t}^{\#}_{2}}^{\prime}
,
which implies that
\mathsf{label}({t}^{\#}_{1})=\mathsf{label}({{t}^{\#}_{1}}^{\prime})=\mathsf{label}(t_{1})\in\mathsf{PInt}
and
\mathsf{label}({t}^{\#}_{2})=\mathsf{label}({{t}^{\#}_{2}}^{\prime})=\mathsf{label}(t_{2})\in\mathsf{PInt}
,
as required.

We proceed now with the converse:
assuming strong observational refinement, we construct a forward simulation.
This is done by first applying strong observational refinement to a “most-general client” program
\mathtt{MGC}
.
In addition to enabling arbitrary invocations of object methods, as one would expect from a most general client, the client program
\mathtt{MGC}
also tosses coins (called “guesses”), that are marked by arbitrary transitions of the implementation.
Intuitively, this lets us capture any non-deterministic behavior of the implementation as a hyperproperty of
\mathtt{MGC}
.

Formally, given an implementation
I
of an object
O
we define

\mathtt{MGC}(I,O)
by:

•

\mathtt{MGC}(I,O).{\mathtt{Q}}=\{{{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}}\}
for an arbitrary state
{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}
.

•

\mathtt{MGC}(I,O).{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}={\mathtt{\hat{q}}}
.

•

\mathtt{MGC}(I,O).{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)\cup\{{\mathtt{guess}(a)\;|\;a\in I.{\mathtt{T}}}\}
. (i.e.,
\mathsf{PInt}=\{{\mathtt{guess}(a)\;|\;a\in I.{\mathtt{T}}}\}
)

•

\mathtt{MGC}(I,O).{\mathtt{T}}=\{{{\langle{{\mathtt{\hat{q}}},\sigma,{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}}\rangle}\;|\;\sigma\in\mathtt{MGC}(I,O).{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}
.

Theorem 44
.

Let
I,{I}^{\#}
be implementations of an object
O
.
If
I\leq_{\mathsf{s}}{I}^{\#}
, then
I\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}{I}^{\#}
.

Proof Sketch.

Assume
I\leq_{\mathsf{s}}{I}^{\#}
and let
P=\mathtt{MGC}(I,O)
.
Consider the following scheduler
S
for
P[I]
:

S(e)=\begin{cases}\{{{\langle{{\langle{\mathsf{pre}(t),{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}}\rangle},\mathtt{guess}(t),{\langle{\mathsf{pre}(t),{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}}\rangle}}\rangle}\;|\;t\in I.{\mathtt{T}}}\}&e=\varepsilon\lor\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])\in I.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\\
\{{{\langle{{\langle{\mathsf{pre}(t),{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}}\rangle},\mathsf{label}(t),{\langle{\mathsf{post}(t),{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}}\rangle}}\rangle}}\}&\mathsf{label}(e[\mathsf{last}])=\mathtt{guess}(t)\end{cases}

This scheduler is clearly deterministic.
Using this scheduler,
we get traces of
P[I]
where each implementation transition (including internal implementation transitions)
is preceded by an internal program transition “guessing” it.
Formally, for an execution
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
let
\mathtt{guess}(e)
be an execution where each transition
e[i]
is replaced with a pair of transitions
{\langle{{\langle{\mathsf{pre}(e[i]),{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}}\rangle},\mathtt{guess}(e[i]),{\langle{\mathsf{pre}(e[i]),{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}}\rangle}}\rangle},{\langle{{\langle{\mathsf{pre}(e[i]),{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}}\rangle},\mathsf{label}(e[i]),{\langle{\mathsf{post}(e[i]),{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}}\rangle}}\rangle}
.
It is straightforward to verify that
\mathtt{guess}(e)\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})
.
Now, by our assumption, there exists a deterministic scheduler
{S}^{\#}

such that
\mathsf{traces}({P[I],S})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}=\mathsf{traces}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
.
As every trace of
P
induces a unique execution of
P
by adding pre-state and post-state
{\mathtt{\hat{q}}}
to each transition label, we deduce that
\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})|_{P}=\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P}
.

Using
Lemma

41
, there exists a bijection

{p}^{\#}:\{{\varepsilon}\}\cup\{{{e}^{\#}\in\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})\;|\;\mathsf{label}({e}^{\#}[\mathsf{last}])\in P.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}\}\to\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P}

defined by
{p}^{\#}({e}^{\#})={e}^{\#}|_{P}
, such that
{{p}^{\#}}^{-1}
is prefix preserving.

We define a mapping
\pi:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathsf{E}({{I}^{\#}})
by

\pi(e)={{p}^{\#}}^{-1}(\mathtt{guess}(e)|_{P})|_{{I}^{\#}}
.
First, observe that indeed
\pi(e)\in\mathsf{E}({{I}^{\#}})
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
:

\displaystyle e\in\mathsf{E}({I})

\displaystyle\implies\mathtt{guess}(e)\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})

\displaystyle\implies\mathtt{guess}(e)|_{P}\in\mathsf{E}({P[I],S})|_{P}=\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})|_{P}

\displaystyle\implies{{p}^{\#}}^{-1}(\mathtt{guess}(e)|_{P})\in\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}],{S}^{\#}})\subseteq\mathsf{E}({P[{I}^{\#}]})

\displaystyle\implies{{p}^{\#}}^{-1}(\mathtt{guess}(e)|_{P})|_{{I}^{\#}}\in\mathsf{E}({{I}^{\#}})

The last implication is due to
Prop.

27
.

Moreover,
\pi
is prefix preserving since
\mathtt{guess}
is prefix preserving,

{{p}^{\#}}^{-1}
is prefix preserving,
and restriction to an induced execution is prefix preserving.
Finally, we note that
\mathsf{trace}({e})|_{\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)}=\mathsf{trace}({\pi(e)})|_{\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)}
(i.e.,
\mathsf{h}({e})=\mathsf{h}({\pi(e)})
) for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
as
\mathtt{guess}
and restrictions to LTSs with an alphabet that includes
\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)
are all operations that respect the restriction to
\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)
.
Overall we obtain a prefix preserving mapping
\pi
that respects
\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)
and due to
Lemma

26
we have
I\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}{I}^{\#}
.
∎

Appendix D
Complete Implementation for the Class
\mathcal{I}_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})

In this section we prove
Lemma

13
.
Let
O=(\mathsf{M},\mathsf{Val})
be an object,
\mathit{Spec}
a specification, and
R
a preorder over sequences.
First we prove downward closure:

Lemma 45
.

\mathcal{I}_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})
is downward closed w.r.t. forward simulation.

Proof.

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})
be an implementation and let
I^{\prime}
be another implementation such that
I^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}I
.
From
I\in\mathcal{I}_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})
there exists a linearization mapping
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}
such that
{\langle{L(e_{1}),L(e_{2})}\rangle}\in R
whenever
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.
From
I^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}I
and
Lemma

26
there exists a mapping
\pi:\mathsf{E}({I^{\prime}})\to\mathsf{E}({I})
such that
\mathsf{h}({e})=\mathsf{h}({\pi(e)})
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({I^{\prime}})

and
\pi(e_{1})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{2})
for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I^{\prime}})
such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.

Define a mapping
L^{\prime}:\mathsf{E}({I^{\prime}})\to\mathit{Spec}
by
L^{\prime}=L\circ\pi
.
We obtain that
L^{\prime}
is a linearization mapping since
\mathsf{h}({e})=\mathsf{h}({\pi(e)})\sqsubseteq L(\pi(e))=L^{\prime}(e)
for all
e\in\mathsf{E}({I^{\prime}})
,
and
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}\implies\pi(e_{1})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\pi(e_{2})\implies{\langle{L(\pi(e_{1})),L(\pi(e_{1})}\rangle}\in R\implies{\langle{L^{\prime}(e_{1}),L^{\prime}(e_{2})}\rangle}\in R
for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I^{\prime}})
such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.
∎

Next, we define an
\mathcal{I}_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})
-complete object
I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})
,
as follows:

•

I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec}).{\mathtt{Q}}=\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)^{*}\times\mathit{Spec}\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to\mathsf{Id}^{*})\\
\text{\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad}\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to\{{\mathtt{main},{\mathtt{b}({\mathit{m}},{v})},{\mathtt{p}(\mathit{m})},{\mathtt{e}({\mathit{m}},{v})}\;|\;\mathit{m}\in\mathsf{M},v\in\mathsf{Val}}\})

(An history, a valid sequential history, used operation IDs for each thread, and a local state of each thread:
\mathtt{main}
denotes no pending method,
{\mathtt{b}({\mathit{m}},{v})}
denotes that a method of type
\mathit{m}
with argument
v
was invoked with the invocation not yet “logged” in the history,
{\mathtt{p}(\mathit{m})}
denotes that a method of type
\mathit{m}
is pending, and
{\mathtt{e}({\mathit{m}},{v})}
denotes that a method of type
\mathit{m}
with return value
v
had its response “logged” in the history and has not responded yet.)

•

I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec}).{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)\cup\{{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}\;|\;p\in\mathsf{Tid}}\}

•

I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec}).{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}={\langle{\varepsilon,\varepsilon,\lambda p\ldotp\varepsilon,\lambda p\ldotp\mathtt{main}}\rangle}

•

The transitions are as follows:

\frac{i=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathit{m},v,p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{St}(p)=\mathtt{main}\quad k\notin ID(p)\quad\quad ID^{\prime}=ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k]\quad\quad\mathit{St}^{\prime}=\mathit{St}[p\mapsto{\mathtt{b}({\mathit{m}},{v})}]}{{\langle{h,s,ID,\mathit{St}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{i}$}}{}{\langle{h,s,ID^{\prime},\mathit{St}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{inv}}\frac{i=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathit{m},v,p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{St}(p)={\mathtt{b}({\mathit{m}},{v})}\quad k=ID(p)[\mathsf{last}]\quad\quad h\cdot i\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}\quad{\langle{s,s^{\prime}}\rangle}\in R\quad\quad h^{\prime}=h\cdot i\quad\mathit{St}^{\prime}=\mathit{St}[p\mapsto{\mathtt{p}(\mathit{m})}]}{{\langle{h,s,ID,\mathit{St}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}{\langle{h^{\prime},s^{\prime},ID,\mathit{St}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{log-inv}}\frac{r=\mathtt{res}{\langle{\mathit{m},v,p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{St}(p)={\mathtt{p}(\mathit{m})}\quad k=ID(p)[\mathsf{last}]\quad\quad h\cdot r\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}\quad{\langle{s,s^{\prime}}\rangle}\in R\quad\quad h^{\prime}=h\cdot r\quad\mathit{St}^{\prime}=\mathit{St}[p\mapsto{\mathtt{e}({\mathit{m}},{v})}]}{{\langle{h,s,ID,\mathit{St}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}{\langle{h^{\prime},s^{\prime},ID,\mathit{St}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{log-res}}\frac{r=\mathtt{res}{\langle{\mathit{m},v,p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{St}(p)={\mathtt{e}({\mathit{m}},{v})}\quad k=ID(p)[\mathsf{last}]\quad\quad\mathit{St}^{\prime}=\mathit{St}[p\mapsto\mathtt{main}]}{{\langle{h,s,ID,\mathit{St}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{r}$}}{}{\langle{h,s,ID,\mathit{St}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{res}}

Theorem 46
.

I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})
is
\mathcal{I}_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})
-complete

Proof.

For inclusion
I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})\in\mathcal{I}_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})
,
let
{\langle{h_{e},s_{e},ID_{e},\mathit{St}_{e}}\rangle}
denote the state reached by an execution
e\in\mathsf{E}({I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})})
(i.e., the initial state if
e=\varepsilon
and otherwise
\mathsf{post}(e)
).
By transitivity of
R
we have that
{\langle{s_{e_{1}},s_{e_{2}}}\rangle}\in R
for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})})
such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.

Let
E
be the set of executions such that every thread is either outside a method or in a “pending state” in the state they reach.
Formally:

E\triangleq\{{e\in\mathsf{E}({I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})})\;|\;\forall p\in\mathsf{Tid}\ldotp\mathit{St}_{e}(p)\in\{{\mathtt{main}}\}\cup\{{{\mathtt{p}(\mathit{m})}\;|\;\mathit{m}\in\mathsf{M}.}\}}\}

It is straightforward to verify that
h_{e}=\mathsf{h}({e})
and
h_{e}\sqsubseteq s_{e}
for all
e\in E
.

Next, we claim there exists a prefix preserving mapping
g:\mathsf{E}({I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})})\to E

such that
\mathsf{h}({e})=\mathsf{h}({g(e)})
for all
e\in\mathsf{E}({I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})})
.

Indeed, given an execution
e\in\mathsf{E}({I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})})

and a transition
t
such that
e\cdot t\in\mathsf{E}({I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})})
and
\mathsf{label}(t)\in\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)
,
let
t_{\ell}
be a transition such that:

•

If
\mathsf{label}(t)\in\mathsf{I}(O)
, then
t_{\ell}
is the transition using the rule
log-inv
that becomes available following the transition
t
in the execution
e\cdot t
.

•

If
\mathsf{label}(t)\in\mathsf{R}(O)
, then
t_{\ell}
is the transition using the rule
log-res
such that
t
becomes available following the transition
t_{\ell}
in the execution
e\cdot t_{\ell}
.

We define
g
inductively by
g(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon
and:

g(e\cdot t)=\begin{cases}g(e)&\mathsf{label}(t)=\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}\\
g(e)\cdot t\cdot t_{\ell}&\mathsf{label}(t)\in\mathsf{I}(O)\\
g(e)\cdot t_{\ell}\cdot t&\mathsf{label}(t)\in\mathsf{R}(O)\end{cases}

It is straightforward to verify that
g
returns executions in
E
and that it satisfies all required properties.

Now,
we define a linearization mapping
L:\mathsf{E}({I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})})\to\mathit{Spec}
by
L(e)=s_{g(e)}
.
We obtain that
\mathsf{h}({e})=\mathsf{h}({g(e)})=h_{g(e)}\sqsubseteq s_{g(e)}=L(e)
for all
e\in\mathsf{E}({I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})})
,
and that
g(e_{1})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}g(e_{2})
and thus
{\langle{L(e_{1}),L(e_{2})}\rangle}={\langle{s_{g(e_{1})},s_{g(e_{2})}}\rangle}\in R
for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})})
such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.

For hardness, let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})
be an implementation, and
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}
a linearization mapping such that
{\langle{L(e_{1}),L(e_{2})}\rangle}\in R
whenever
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.

Let
d:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathsf{E}({I})
be a mapping taking each
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
to its maximal prefix ending in an invocation or response transition,
and denote
L^{\prime}=L\circ d
.
It is straightforward to verify that
L^{\prime}:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}
is also a linearization mapping such that
{\langle{L^{\prime}(e_{1}),L^{\prime}(e_{2})}\rangle}\in R
whenever
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
,
with the additional property that
L(e)=L(e\cdot t)
whenever
\mathsf{label}(t)\notin\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(O)
.

By
Prop.

25
we have that
I\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}{{I}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
, and it suffices to prove that
{{I}^{\mathsf{ex}}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})
.
For this we use the following simulation relation:

\left\{{\langle{e,{\langle{\mathsf{h}({e}),L^{\prime}(e),ID^{e},\mathit{St}^{e}}\rangle}}\rangle}\;|\;e\in\mathsf{E}({I})\right\}

where for each
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
,
ID^{e}(p)
is the sequence of identifiers used for operations in
\mathsf{h}({e})|_{p}
,
and
\mathit{St}^{e}(p)
is
{\mathtt{p}(\mathit{m})}
if
p
is within a pending method
\mathit{m}
at the state reached by
e
, and
\mathtt{main}
otherwise.

A simple case analysis over
\mathsf{label}(t)
shows that given a pair of states
{\langle{e,{\langle{\mathsf{h}({e}),L^{\prime}(e),ID^{e},\mathit{St}^{e}}\rangle}}\rangle}
and a transition
e{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathsf{label}(t)}$}_{{{I}^{\mathsf{ex}}}}}{}e\cdot t
,
there exists a sequence of transitions

{\langle{\mathsf{h}({e}),L^{\prime}(e),ID^{e},\mathit{St}^{e}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{${\mathrel{\to}^{\mathsf{label}(t)}}$}_{I_{R}(O,\mathit{Spec})}}{}{\langle{\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t}),L^{\prime}(e\cdot t),ID^{e\cdot t},\mathit{St}^{e\cdot t}}\rangle}
.
∎

Appendix E
Definitions of Complete Implementations

We present the formal definitions of the complete implementation’s LTSs.
In both implementations, each thread’s state corresponds to a location in the pseudocode,
and includes the values of shared and local variables.
Transitions represent parts of the code that are executed atomically.

We also prove that each implementation is included in the appropriate linearizability class.
Their hardness is proved in
Appendix

F
.

E.1
Complete Implementation for Write Strong Registers:
\mathtt{WSR}

Definition of
\mathtt{WSR}
:

•

\mathit{PC}_{\text{WS}}=\{{\mathtt{main},\mathtt{w_{b}},\mathtt{w_{e}},\mathtt{r_{b}},\mathtt{r_{l}},\mathtt{r_{e}}}\}
(locations in the pseudocode from
Algorithm

4
)

•

\mathtt{WSR}.{\mathtt{Q}}=\mathsf{Val}\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to\mathsf{Id}^{*})\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to\mathit{PC}_{\text{WS}})\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to\mathcal{P}({\mathsf{Val}}))\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to\mathsf{Val})
(register value, used operation IDs, program counter, set of values each read collected, input/output argument of the method)

•

\mathtt{WSR}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(\mathsf{Reg})\cup\{{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}\;|\;p\in\mathsf{Tid}}\}

•

\mathtt{WSR}.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}={\langle{0,\lambda p\ldotp\varepsilon,\lambda p\ldotp\mathtt{main},\lambda p\ldotp\emptyset,\lambda p\ldotp 0}\rangle}

•

The transitions are as follows:

\frac{a=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{read},\bot,p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{main}\quad k\notin ID(p)\quad\quad ID^{\prime}=ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k]\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{r_{b}}]}{{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,ID^{\prime},\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{read-inv}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{r_{b}}\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{r_{l}}]\quad\quad\mathit{VS}^{\prime}=\mathit{VS}[p\mapsto\{{v}\}]}{{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{read-init}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{r_{l}}\quad v\notin\mathit{VS}(p)\quad\quad\mathit{VS}^{\prime}=\mathit{VS}[p\mapsto\mathit{VS}(p)\uplus\{{v}\}]}{{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{read-loop}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{r_{l}}\quad v_{\text{out}}\in\mathit{VS}(p)\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{r_{e}}]\quad\quad\mathit{A}^{\prime}=\mathit{A}[p\mapsto v_{\text{out}}]}{{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{read-pick}}\frac{a=\mathtt{res}{\langle{\mathtt{read},\mathit{A}(p),p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{r_{e}}\quad k=ID(p)[\mathsf{last}]\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{main}]\quad\quad\mathit{VS}^{\prime}=\mathit{VS}[p\mapsto\emptyset]\quad\quad\mathit{A}^{\prime}=\mathit{A}[p\mapsto 0]}{{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{read-res}}\frac{a=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{write},v_{\text{in}},p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{main}\quad k\notin ID(p)\quad\quad ID^{\prime}=ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k]\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{b}}]\quad\quad\mathit{A}^{\prime}=\mathit{A}[p\mapsto v_{\text{in}}]}{{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,ID^{\prime},\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{write-inv}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{w_{b}}\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}]\quad\quad v^{\prime}=\mathit{A}(p)}{{\langle{\_,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v^{\prime},ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{write-int}}\frac{a=\mathtt{res}{\langle{\mathtt{write},\bot,p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{w_{e}}\quad k=ID(p)[\mathsf{last}]\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{main}]\quad\quad\mathit{VS}^{\prime}=\mathit{VS}[p\mapsto\emptyset]\quad\quad\mathit{A}^{\prime}=\mathit{A}[p\mapsto 0]}{{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{write-res}}

We show that
\mathtt{WSR}
is indeed write strongly-linearizable.
The idea is to linearize write operations that already stored their value (in a
write-int
transition) and read operations that already picked a value to return (in a
read-pick
transition).
Intuitively, these transitions are when operations “decide” their relative ordering w.r.t already linearized operations.
The linearized operations are ordered by marking linearization points.

Theorem 47
.

\mathtt{WSR}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
.

Proof.

The write strong-linearization
L
for
\mathtt{WSR}
is constructed as follows.
Given an execution
e
,
let
W(e)
be the set of all write operations that performed a
write-int
transition in
e
, where pending write operations are completed with a matching response.
Similarly, let
R(e)
be the set of all read operations that performed a
read-pick
transition, where pending read operations are completed with a matching response containing the value that was picked in said transition.

Let
S(e)=W(e)\cup R(e)
and define a mapping
lin:S(e)\to\{{t\;|\;t\in e}\}
by:

1.

If
o\in S(e)
is a write operation, then
lin(o)
is the operation’s
write-int
transition.

2.

If
o\in S(e)
is a read operation, then
lin(o)
is the
read-loop
transition where it loads the value it later picks in its
read-pick
transition.

We define
L(e)
to be the sequence over
S(e)
induced by
lin
, i.e.,
o_{1}<_{L(e)}o_{2}
iff
lin(o_{1})<_{e}lin(o_{2})
.
We prove that all required properties are satisfied:

•

L(e)\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
:

Follows from the fact the last write (if any) before each read in
L(e)
is the last write that stored in a
write-int
transition before the read loaded the value in a
read-loop
transition and later picked it in a
read-pick
transition, hence that read’s output in
L(e)
is equal to the input of said write (or the initial value, if no such write exists).

•

\mathsf{h}({e})\sqsubseteq L(e)
:

We show the conditions in
Lemma

31
are satisfied.
Let
\bar{r}
be a subsequence of
L(e)
composed of response for operations in
L(e)
that are pending in
\mathsf{h}({e})
.
Denoting
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e})\cdot\bar{r})
, we have as required:

–

\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})=\mathsf{set}(L(e))
:

Let
o\in\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})
.
If
o\in\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e}))
, then it already performed either a
write-int
transition or a
read-pick
transition, and thus
o\in L(e)
.
Otherwise,
\bar{r}
contains the response of
o
which implies by definition that
o\in L(e)
.

For the converse, let
o\in L(e)
.
If
o\in\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e}))
, then
\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e}))\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e})\cdot\bar{r})
and thus
o\in\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})
.
Otherwise, the response of
o
is in
\bar{r}
by definition,
and so again
o\in\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})
.

–

<_{h^{\prime}}\;\subseteq\;<_{L(e)}
:

Let
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})
be operations such that
o_{1}<_{h^{\prime}}o_{2}
.
Let
r_{1}
be
o_{1}
’s response and let
i_{2}
be
o_{2}
’s invocation.
Then we have that
lin(o_{1})<_{e}r_{1}<_{e}i_{2}<_{e}lin(o_{2})

and so
o_{1}<_{L(e)}o_{2}
as required.

•

L
is write strong:

Let
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I})
be executions such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.
Then
L(e_{1})|_{\mathtt{write}}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}}

follows from the fact that writes in
L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}}
that are not in
L(e_{1})|_{\mathtt{write}}

all perform their
write-int
transition after all transitions in
e_{1}
are executed. ∎

E.2
Complete Implementation for Decisive Registers:
\mathtt{DR}

Definition of
\mathtt{DR}
:

•

\mathit{PC}_{\text{D}}=\{{\mathtt{main},\mathtt{w_{b}},\mathtt{w_{w}},\mathtt{w_{r}},\mathtt{w_{e}},\mathtt{r_{b}},\mathtt{r_{l}},\mathtt{r_{e}}}\}

•

\mathtt{DR}.{\mathtt{Q}}=\mathsf{Val}\times{\mathbb{N}}\times\{{0,1}\}\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to\mathsf{Id}^{*})\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to\mathit{PC}_{\text{D}})\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to\mathcal{P}({\mathsf{Val}}))\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to{\mathbb{N}})\times(\mathsf{Tid}\to\mathsf{Val})
(Register value, version, lock, used operation IDs, program counter, set of values, input/output argument of the method (also used to hold a temporary value))

•

\mathtt{DR}.{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(\mathsf{Reg})\cup\{{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}\;|\;p\in\mathsf{Tid}}\}

•

\mathtt{DR}.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}={\langle{0,0,0,\lambda p\ldotp\varepsilon,\lambda p\ldotp\mathtt{main},\lambda p\ldotp\emptyset,\lambda p\ldotp 0,\lambda p\ldotp 0}\rangle}

•

The transitions are as follows:

\frac{a=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{read},\bot,p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{main}\quad k\notin ID(p)\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{r_{b}}]\quad\quad ID^{\prime}=ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k]}{{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID^{\prime},\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{read-inv}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{r_{b}}\quad\ell=0\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{r_{l}}]\quad\quad\mathit{VS}^{\prime}=\mathit{VS}[p\mapsto\{{v}\}]\quad\quad\mathit{S}^{\prime}=\mathit{S}[p\mapsto\mathit{Ver}]}{{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{S}^{\prime},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{read-init}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{r_{l}}\quad v\notin\mathit{VS}(p)\quad\quad\mathit{Ver}\geq\mathit{S}(p)\quad\quad\mathit{VS}^{\prime}=\mathit{VS}[p\mapsto\mathit{VS}(p)\uplus\{{v}\}]}{{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{read-loop}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{r_{l}}\quad v_{\text{out}}\in\mathit{VS}(p)\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{r_{e}}]\quad\quad\mathit{A}^{\prime}=\mathit{A}[p\mapsto v_{\text{out}}]}{{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{read-pick}}\frac{a=\mathtt{res}{\langle{\mathtt{read},\mathit{A}(p),p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{r_{e}}\quad k=ID(p)[\mathsf{last}]\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{main}]\quad\quad\mathit{VS}^{\prime}=\mathit{VS}[p\mapsto\emptyset]\quad\quad\mathit{S}^{\prime}=\mathit{S}[p\mapsto 0]\quad\quad\mathit{A}^{\prime}=\mathit{A}[p\mapsto 0]}{{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{S}^{\prime},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{read-res}}\frac{a=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{write},v_{\text{in}},p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{main}\quad k\notin ID(p)\quad\quad ID^{\prime}=ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k]\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{b}}]\quad\quad\mathit{A}^{\prime}=\mathit{A}[p\mapsto v_{\text{in}}]}{{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}}{}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID^{\prime},\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{write-inv}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{w_{b}}\quad\ell=0\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{w}}]\quad\quad\mathit{S}^{\prime}=\mathit{S}[p\mapsto\mathit{Ver}]}{{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S}^{\prime},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{write-init}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{w_{w}}\quad\ell=0\quad\quad v^{\prime}=\mathit{A}(p)\quad\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}=\mathit{Ver}+1\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}]}{{\langle{\_,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v^{\prime},\mathit{Ver}^{\prime},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{write-top}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{w_{w}}\quad\mathit{Ver}>\mathit{S}(p)\quad\ell=0\quad\quad v^{\prime}=\mathit{A}(p)\quad\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}=\mathit{Ver}-1\quad\quad\ell^{\prime}=1\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{r}}]\quad\quad\mathit{A}^{\prime}=\mathit{A}[p\mapsto v]}{{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v^{\prime},\mathit{Ver}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{write-roll-back}}\frac{\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{w_{r}}\quad\ell=1\quad\quad v^{\prime}=\mathit{A}(p)\quad\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}=\mathit{Ver}+1\quad\quad\ell^{\prime}=0\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}]}{{\langle{\_,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}}{}\\
\\
\qquad{\langle{v^{\prime},\mathit{Ver}^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{write-roll-forward}}\frac{a=\mathtt{res}{\langle{\mathtt{write},\bot,p,k}\rangle}\quad\quad\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{w_{e}}\quad k=ID(p)[\mathsf{last}]\quad\quad\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{main}]\quad\quad\mathit{VS}^{\prime}=\mathit{VS}[p\mapsto\emptyset]\quad\quad\mathit{S}^{\prime}=\mathit{S}[p\mapsto 0]\quad\quad\mathit{A}^{\prime}=\mathit{A}[p\mapsto 0]}{{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}}{}\\
\\
