\qquad{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{S}^{\prime},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{write-res}}

We show that
\mathtt{DR}
is indeed decisively linearizable.
The set of operations to linearize is chosen similarly to the proof of
Thm.

47
: writes that already stored their value and reads that already picked a value to return.
The ordering of these operations is more involved in this case,
as it is less clear how to assign linearization points.
Specifically, for writes that choose to be “overwritten” by a concurrent write, their linearization point should be somewhere between their invocation and the linearization point of the write that overwrote them,
but it is not immediately clear how to choose a specific transition within that interval.
Instead of resolving this question directly, we order the operations based on a version number associated with each, and use the relative order of specific transitions only to resolve version number ties.

Theorem 48
.

\mathtt{DR}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{d}}
.

Proof.

The decisive linearization
L
for
\mathtt{WSR}
is constructed as follows.
Given an execution
e
,
let
W(e)
be the set of all write operations that performed
either a
write-top
or a
write-roll-back
transition in
e
, where pending write operations are completed with a matching response.
Let
R(e)
be the set of all read operations that performed a
read-pick
transition, where pending read operations are completed with a matching response containing the value they picked in said transition.

Denote
S(e)=W(e)\cup R(e)
, and define mappings
f:S(e)\to e
,
ver:S(e)\to{\mathbb{N}}
, by:

1.

If
o\in S(e)
is a write operation, then
f(o)
is either the
write-top
or the
write-roll-back
transition performed by
o
in
e
,
and
ver(o)
is the version number stored to
\mathit{Ver}
in said transition.

2.

If
o\in S(e)
is a read operation, then
f(o)
is the
read-loop
transition where it loads the value it later picks in its
read-pick
transition,
and
ver(o)
is the value of
\mathit{Ver}
in the state when said
read-loop
transition is performed.

We define
L(e)
to be the sequence over
S(e)
induced by
ver
, using
f
for tie-breaking.
i.e.,
o_{1}<_{L(e)}o_{2}
if and only if either
ver(o_{1})<ver(o_{2})
, or
ver(o_{1})=ver(o_{2})
and

f(o_{1})<_{e}f(o_{2})
.

Note that
the leftmost operation in
L(e)
associated with any version number greater than 0 is the write operation that stored this number in a
write-top
transition, and all other writes associated with the same version number are writes that stored this number later in
e
in a
write-roll-back
transition.
In particular, all write operations are mapped to a version number strictly greater than
0
.

We prove that all required properties are satisfied:

•

L(e)\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
:

Let
r\in L(e)
be a read operation.
We need to show

{\mathtt{output}}(r)=\mathtt{obs}(\mathtt{pref}_{L(e)}({o}))
.

Equivalently, defining
w_{1}=\mathsf{last_{op}}(\mathtt{pref}_{L(e)}({r})|_{\mathtt{write}})
(i.e., the last write operation before
r
in
L(e)
, if such exists, and otherwise
\varepsilon
),
we need to show that
{\mathtt{output}}(r)={\mathtt{input}}(w_{1})
if
w_{1}\neq\varepsilon
and
{\mathtt{output}}(r)=0
if
w_{1}=\varepsilon
.

Let
w_{2}
be a write operation such that
f(w_{2})<_{e}f(r)
,

f(w_{2})
is either a
write-top
transition or a
write-roll-back
transition with no matching
write-roll-forward
transition before the transition
f(r)
,
and there is no write operation
w^{\prime}
satisfying this condition such that
f(w_{2})<_{e}f(w^{\prime})
,
or
w_{2}=\varepsilon
if no such write exists.

Consider two cases:

–

If
w_{2}\neq\varepsilon
:

By definition of
w_{2}
we clearly have
f(w_{2})<_{e}f(r)
,
ver(w_{2})=ver(r)
, and

{\mathtt{output}}(r)={\mathtt{input}}(w_{2})
.
Therefore,
w_{2}<_{L(e)}r
.
By definition of
w_{1}
this implies that
w_{1}\neq\varepsilon
and in particular
w_{2}\leq_{L(e)}w_{1}
.
As
w_{1}<_{L(e)}r
and
ver(w_{2})=ver(r)
we obtain that
ver(w_{1})=ver(r)

and so
f(w_{1})<_{e}f(r)
.
Consider two cases:

*

If
f(w_{1})
is a
write-roll-back
transition with a matching
write-roll-forward
transition before
f(r)
in
e
,
that
write-roll-forward
transition’s post-state has a version number larger than
ver(w_{1})=ver(r)
,
which implies
r
loads in the transition
f(r)
a value written later by an additional
write-roll-back
transition of a write operation
w^{\prime}
with no matching response before
f(r)
.
We deduce from definition of
w_{2}
that
w^{\prime}=w_{2}
and as
f(w_{1})<_{e}f(w^{\prime})
we obtain that
f(w_{1})<_{e}f(w_{2})
.

*

Otherwise,
f(w_{1})
is either a
write-top
transition, or a
write-roll-back
transition with no matching
write-roll-forward
transition before
f(r)
in
e
.
In both cases we deduce from the definition of
w_{2}
that necessarily
f(w_{1})\leq_{e}f(w_{2})
.

We obtain that
f(w_{1})\leq_{e}f(w_{2})
and
ver(w_{1})=ver(w_{2})
and thus
w_{1}\leq_{L(e)}w_{2}
and overall
w_{1}=w_{2}
.
This gives as required that
{\mathtt{output}}(r)={\mathtt{input}}(w_{2})={\mathtt{input}}(w_{1})
.

–

If
w_{2}=\varepsilon
, then clearly
{\mathtt{output}}(r)=0
,
ver(r)=0
,
and it suffices to show
w_{1}=\varepsilon
.
Indeed, if we assume otherwise then from
w_{1}<_{L(e)}r
we obtain that
ver(w_{1})=0
,
contradicting the fact that all write operations are assigned a positive version number.

•

\mathsf{h}({e})\sqsubseteq L(e)
:

We show the conditions in
Lemma

31
are satisfied.
Let
\bar{r}
be a subsequence of
L(e)
composed of response for operations in
L(e)
that are pending in
\mathsf{h}({e})
.
Denoting
h^{\prime}=\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e})\cdot\bar{r})
,
the fact that
\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})=\mathsf{set}(L(e))
is proved exactly like in the proof of
Thm.

47
,
and it remains to show that
<_{h^{\prime}}\;\subseteq\;<_{L(e)}
.
Let
o_{1},o_{2}\in\mathsf{set}(h^{\prime})
be operations such that
o_{1}<_{h^{\prime}}o_{2}
.
First, we show that
ver(o_{1})\leq ver(o_{2})
.
Let
t_{2}
be the transition where
o_{2}
performs either a
read-init
or
write-init
transition.
This is well defined as
o_{2}\in S(e)
implies that
o_{2}
already performed in
e
the transition
f(o_{2})
,
and we have
t_{2}<_{e}f(o_{2})
.
Since
o_{1}
responds before
o_{2}
is invoked in
e
,
we must have that
f(o_{1})<_{e}t_{2}
.
As
t_{2}
is performed under a precondition that
\ell=0
,
there exists a transition
t
such that
f(o_{1})\leq_{e}t<_{e}t_{2}
and

\ell=0
in the post-state of
t
.
Let
t_{1}
be the first (w.r.t.
<_{e}
) such transition.
Let
\mathit{Ver}_{i}
denote the version number in the post-state of transition
t_{i}
.
We have:

–

ver(o_{1})\leq\mathit{Ver}_{1}
:

If
f(o_{1})=t_{1}
, we are done.
Otherwise, the transition
f(o_{1})
is performed after some write performed its
write-roll-back
transition (possibly
f(o_{1})
is exactly this transition), but before that write performed its
write-roll-forward
transition.
It must be the case that
t_{1}
is said
write-roll-forward
transition, as no other operation can update the lock from
1
to
0
.
As no transition prior to
t_{1}
can update the version number,
and it increase by 1 after
t_{1}
, we obtain that
ver(o_{1})<\mathit{Ver}_{1}
.

–

\mathit{Ver}_{1}\leq\mathit{Ver}_{2}
:

Follows from the fact that
t_{1}<_{e}t_{2}
, the version number is monotonically non-decreasing when considering points in the execution where
\ell=0
, and indeed
\ell=0
in the post-states of
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.

–

\mathit{Ver}_{2}\leq ver(o_{2})
:

Consider the following cases:

*

If
f(o_{2})
is a
write-top
transition, this follows from
t_{2}<_{e}f(o_{2})
, using again the fact that the version number is monotonically non-decreasing when considering points in the execution where
\ell=0
, and indeed
\ell=0
in the post-states of
t_{2}
and
f(o_{2})
.

*

If
f(o_{2})
is a
write-roll-back
transition,
then
ver(o_{2})
is smaller by 1 then the version number
\mathit{Ver}
in pre-state of
f(o_{2})
,
and this transition is done under a condition that
\mathit{Ver}
is strictly larger than the version number
o_{2}
read in the
write-init
transition, which is
\mathit{Ver}_{2}
.

*

If
f(o_{2})
is a
read-loop
transition, then
ver(o_{2})
is loaded under a condition that indeed it is larger than or equal to the version number
o_{2}
read in the
read-init
transition, which is
\mathit{Ver}_{2}
.

We obtain that
ver(o_{1})\leq ver(o_{2})
.
Next we prove that
o_{1}<_{L(e)}o_{2}
. Consider the following cases:

–

If
ver(o_{1})<ver(o_{2})
, then by definition
o_{1}<_{L(e)}o_{2}
as required.

–

If
ver(o_{1})=ver(o_{2})
,
let
r_{1}
be
o_{1}
’s response and let
i_{2}
be
o_{2}
’s invocation.
Then we have that
f(o_{1})<_{e}r_{1}<_{e}i_{2}<_{e}f(o_{2})

and so overall
o_{1}<_{L(e)}o_{2}
as required.

•

L
is decisive:

Let
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I})
be executions such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.
We clearly have that
S(e_{1})\subseteq S(e_{2})
as additional transitions can only add operations to
S
.
Moreover, the ordering between operations in
S(e_{1})
is not effected by the additional operations added,
and thus,
L(e_{1})\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}L(e_{2})
.∎

Appendix F
Hardness of Complete Implementations

In this section we prove the hardness of
\mathtt{WSR}
and
\mathtt{DR}
for their perspective linearizability classes.
We being in
Section

F.1
with a motivating discussion.
In
Section

F.2
we formally define “lazy linearizations”: linearizations with additional properties that are useful for the hardness proof, presented in
Section

F.3
.

F.1
Motivation and Proof Strategy

To motivate our approach, we begin by sketching a proof attempt for the
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
-hardness of
\mathtt{WSR}
.

Given a write strong implementation
I
, we wish to prove that
I\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}\mathtt{WSR}
.
Let
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
be a write strong-linearization for
I
.
We begin by attaching to the state of
I
a ghost variable keeping the execution performed so far (see
Definition

24
for more details).
The resulting implementation
{{I}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
is simulation-equivalent to
I
and so it is sufficient to show
{{I}^{\mathsf{ex}}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}\mathtt{WSR}
.
While we have no direct information about the structure of
I
’s state,
given a state up to which an execution
e
was performed, and a transition
t
, we can look at the linearizations
L(e)
and
L(e\cdot t)
, and try to learn the “effect” of the transition
t
from the difference between them.

A first attempt to simulate
{{I}^{\mathsf{ex}}}
is to match the invocations and responses, and perform in
\mathtt{WSR}
the internal step of writes in response to a transition
t
after which they first appear in the linearization
L(e\cdot t)
.
For reads, we can load the currently stored value every time it is changed to a value not loaded in previous transitions, in the hope that when the reads appear in the linearization, the return value described in that linearization is included in the set of values loaded by the read method, which would allow us to simulate the response of that read.

This attempt fails. To show this, consider the following register implementation:

Algorithm 6

​​ Ticket Protocol

Shared Variables:

the current register value
X
, a counter
C_{1}
for tickets taken by writers, and a counter
C_{2}
for executed writes.

2

Method

read(
)

\mathit{out}\leftarrow X
;

return

\mathit{out}
;

Method

write(
v
)

{\langle{t,C_{1}}\rangle}\leftarrow{\langle{C_{1},C_{1}+1}\rangle}
;

wait until

t=C_{2}
;

{\langle{X,C_{2}}\rangle}\leftarrow{\langle{v,C_{2}+1}\rangle}
;

return

;

In this implementation, each write takes a ticket from an increasing shared counter
C_{1}
, and later stores its input value when a second shared counter
C_{2}
reaches its ticket number (initially
C_{1}=C_{2}=0
).

This implementation is write strong. A possible write strong linearization mapping for it includes in the linearization all writes that “took a ticket”, ordered according to their ticket number, and linearizes reads after the write which stored the value they loaded.

For the simulation attempt described above, when a write is invoked and takes a ticket, it appears in the linearization and thus we do an internal write step in
\mathtt{WSR}
.
As the write only took a ticket and did not store yet, it is then possible for a newly invoked read to load an older value, and we will not be able to simulate this with a matching newly invoked read in
\mathtt{WSR}
.
The problem here is that writes were added to the linearization mapping before their effect was “final” in the sense of preventing reads from loading older values.

Our solution to this issue is to base the simulation on what we call a
lazy
linearization mapping. Informally, this is a linearization mapping that only puts operations in the linearization of some execution when it
must
, either because the operation returned, or since it is necessary for validity of the sequential history (i.e. a write before a read returning its value), or for satisfying the prefix condition of write strong linearizability w.r.t linearizations of shorter executions.

When using a lazy linearization, the sequence of linearized write operations describes more accurately what values invoked read operations might return in the future,
and the simulation sketch described above can be proven correct.

F.2
Lazy Linearizations

We define the following properties:

Definition 49
.

Let
I
be an implementation,
\mathit{Spec}
be a specification,
and
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}
be a linearization mapping.

L
is called:
2
2

2

The last two properties
only apply when
I
is a register implementation and
\mathit{Spec}=\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
.

•

response-activated
if
L(e)=L(e\cdot t)
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
and
t\in I.{\mathtt{T}}
such that
e\cdot t\in\mathsf{E}({I})
and
t\notin\mathsf{R}
.
(The linearization remains the same when appending a non-response transition to an execution.)

•

last-completed
if
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e))\in\mathsf{set}(\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e})))
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})\setminus\{{\varepsilon}\}
. (The last operation in any non-empty linearization is completed in the linearized execution.)

•

pending-read-free
if
\mathsf{set}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{read}})\subseteq\mathsf{set}(\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e})))
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
. (Every linearized read operation is completed in the linearized execution.)

•

lazy
if
L
is decisive, response-activated, last-completed, and pending-read-free.

The idea behind the last-completed and pending-read-free properties is that a linearization that is “lazy” will not end with a pending operation,
or contain a pending read, as a sequence where such an operation is removed is also a valid linearization of the same execution.

The idea behind the response-activated and decisive properties is that a lazy linearization does not “switch around” operations once they are linearized, and in particular keeps the exact same linearization when that is possible.

In
Appendix

G
, we prove the following theorems:

Theorem 50
.

If
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
, then there exists a lazy write strong-linearization mapping
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
.

Theorem 51
.

If
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{d}}
, then there exists a lazy linearization mapping
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
.

Note that a corollary of
Thm.

50
is that every write strong-linearizable implementation is also decisively linearizable.

The following theorem presents the main useful property of lazy linearizations:
for every read operation in a linearization,
its return value is the input of some write that was added to the linearization after the read was invoked, or was the latest write in the linearization at the time of invocation.
(If no such write exists, the initial value is possibly returned.)

To formalize this claim, we first define the set of values “observable between” two sequential histories.

Definition 52
.

The set of values
observable between
two sequential histories
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
such that
s_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}s_{2}
,
denoted by
\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(s_{1},s_{2})
, is defined as follows:

\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(s_{1},s_{2})=\{{\mathtt{obs}(s_{1})}\}\cup\{{{\mathtt{input}}(w)\;|\;w\in s_{2}|_{\mathtt{write}}\land\forall\hat{w}\in s_{1}|_{\mathtt{write}}\ldotp\hat{w}<_{s_{2}}w}\}

The idea is that if
s_{1}=L(e_{1})
is the lazy linearization of an execution
e_{1}
up to the invocation of some read,
and
s_{2}=L(e_{2})
where
e_{2}
a continuation of
e_{1}
up to a response of that read,
then that read always returns a value in
\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(s_{1},s_{2})
.
Formally, we prove the following:

Theorem 53
.

If
I
is a register implementation,
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
is a decisive and last-completed linearization mapping,

e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
is an execution,

r\in L(e)|_{\mathtt{read}}
is a read operation,
and
e^{\prime}
is the prefix of
e
up to the invocation of
r
(exclusive),
then
{\mathtt{output}}(r)\in\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(e^{\prime}),L(e))
.

In other words,
either the read operation
r
returns the value that can be read after
L(e^{\prime})
, the linearization of the execution up to its invocation,
or it returns the input of some write in
L(e)
which appears after all writes which were already present in
L(e^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By
Definition

37
we have
{\mathtt{output}}(r)=\mathtt{obs}(\mathtt{pref}_{L(e)}({r}))
.
Thus it is sufficient to assume

\mathtt{obs}(\mathtt{pref}_{L(e)}({r}))\notin\{{{\mathtt{input}}(w)\;|\;w\in L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}}\land\forall\hat{w}\in L(e^{\prime})|_{\mathtt{write}}\ldotp\hat{w}<_{L(e)}w}\}

(1)

and prove that
\mathtt{obs}(\mathtt{pref}_{L(e)}({r}))=\mathtt{obs}(L(e^{\prime}))
.

Denote
w=\mathsf{last_{op}}(\mathtt{pref}_{L(e)}({r})|_{\mathtt{write}})
,
w^{\prime}=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e^{\prime})|_{\mathtt{write}})
,
and
o^{\prime}=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e^{\prime}))
.

By definition of
\mathtt{obs}
it is sufficient to show that
w=w^{\prime}
. (Possibly both are
\varepsilon
, if the relevant sequences contain no writes.)
Consider the following cases:

•

If
w\neq\varepsilon
, then

w\in\mathtt{pref}_{L(e)}({r})
and we obtain that
w\in L(e)
. By definition,
\mathtt{obs}(\mathtt{pref}_{L(e)}({r}))={\mathtt{input}}(w)
.
Thus, from assumption (
1
) we obtain that there must exist some
\hat{w}\in L(e^{\prime})
such that
w\leq_{L(e)}\hat{w}
.
As
\hat{w}\in L(e^{\prime})
,
using the definition of
w^{\prime}
we obtain
\hat{w}\leq_{L(e^{\prime})}w^{\prime}
.
Since
L
is decisive,
L(e^{\prime})\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}L(e)

and we obtain that
\hat{w}\leq_{L(e)}w^{\prime}
and overall
w\leq_{L(e)}w^{\prime}
.
We also obtain in particular that
w^{\prime},o^{\prime}\neq\varepsilon
, and so by definition we have

w^{\prime}\leq_{L(e^{\prime})}o^{\prime}
, which similarly to the above implies
w^{\prime}\leq_{L(e)}o^{\prime}
.
As
o^{\prime}=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e^{\prime}))
and
L
is last-completed, we must have
o^{\prime}\in\mathsf{set}(\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e^{\prime}})))
.
That is,
o^{\prime}
is completed in
e^{\prime}
and as
r
is not yet invoked in
e^{\prime}
we obtain that
o^{\prime}<_{\mathsf{h}({e})}r
and thus
o^{\prime}<_{L(e)}r
.
We deduce that
w^{\prime}\leq_{L(e)}r
and so by definition of
w
we obtain that
w^{\prime}\leq_{L(e)}w
.
As we have already shown
w\leq_{L(e)}w^{\prime}
we deduce that
w=w^{\prime}
.

•

If
w=\varepsilon
, then to show
w^{\prime}=\varepsilon
it is sufficient to show that
L(e^{\prime})|_{\mathtt{write}}=\varepsilon
.
Indeed, if we assume otherwise, there exists some
\hat{w}\in L(e^{\prime})
.
Like we did in the previous case, we can deduce that
\hat{w}\leq_{L(e)}w^{\prime}
and thus
w^{\prime},o^{\prime}\neq\varepsilon
.
From that we can deduce exactly like above that
w^{\prime}\leq_{L(e)}r
, contradicting the fact that
w=\varepsilon
and so no write operation appears before
r
in
L(e)
.
We obtain that
w^{\prime}=\varepsilon=w
, as required. ∎

F.3
Hardness Proof

We begin by proving the
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{d}}
-hardness of
\mathtt{DR}
.
Following this proof, we explain how the proof is modified to demonstrate
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
-hardness of
\mathtt{WSR}
.

Theorem 54
.

\mathtt{DR}
is
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{d}}
-hard.

Proof.

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{d}}
be a decisively linearizable register.
By
Thm.

51
, there exists a lazy linearization mapping

L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
.
Using
Prop.

25
, it suffices to prove
{{I}^{\mathsf{ex}}}\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{F}}\mathtt{DR}
.

Given an execution
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
and a thread
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
, we write
i^{e}_{p}
to denote the single pending invocation of thread
p
in
\mathsf{h}({e})
, or
\varepsilon
if such an invocation does not exist.
We abuse notation and write
\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p})
to denote
\mathtt{pref}_{e}({t})
where
t=\varepsilon
if
i^{e}_{p}=\varepsilon
, and otherwise
t\in e|_{p}
is the transition such that
\mathsf{label}(t)=i^{e}_{p}
.

A simulation relation
R
is defined by

{\langle{e,{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\rangle}\in R
iff
the following conditions hold:

(R1)

v=\mathtt{obs}(L(e))
.

(R2)

\ell=0
.

(R3)

For every
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
and
k\in\mathsf{Id}
,
k\in ID(p)\iff\exists a\in\mathsf{h}({e})|_{p}\ldotp{\mathtt{id}}(a)=k
.

(R4)

0\leq\mathit{S}(p)\leq\mathit{Ver}
for every
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
.

(R5)

For every
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
, if
\mathit{S}(p)=\mathit{Ver}
, then
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p}))|_{\mathtt{write}})
.

(R6)

For every
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
, denoting
k=ID(p)[\mathsf{last}]
, one of the following holds:

(R6a)

\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{main}
and
i^{e}_{p}=\varepsilon
.

(R6b)

\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{r_{l}}
,
i^{e}_{p}=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{read},\bot,p,k}\rangle}
,
i^{e}_{p}\in\mathsf{h}({e})
,
i^{e}_{p}\notin L(e)
, and

\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p})),L(e))\subseteq\mathit{VS}(p)
.

(R6c)

\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{w_{w}}
,
i^{e}_{p}=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{write},\mathit{A}(p),p,k}\rangle}
,
i^{e}_{p}\in\mathsf{h}({e})
, and
i^{e}_{p}\notin L(e)
.

(R6d)

\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{w_{e}}
and
i^{e}_{p}=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{write},\_,p,k}\rangle}\in L(e)
.

Clearly, for the initial states we have

{\langle{\varepsilon,\mathtt{DR}.{\mathtt{q_{\mathtt{0}}}}}\rangle}\in R
.

Given states
{\langle{q,{q}^{\#}}\rangle}={\langle{e,{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}}\rangle}\in R

and a transition
e{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}_{{{I}^{\mathsf{ex}}}}}{}e\cdot t=q^{\prime}
where
a=\mathsf{label}(t)
, we present a sequence of transitions
{q}^{\#}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{${\mathrel{\to}^{\bar{a}}}$}_{\mathtt{DR}}}{}{{q}^{\#}}^{\prime}
such that
a|_{\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(\mathsf{Reg})}=\bar{a}|_{\mathsf{I}\mathsf{R}(\mathsf{Reg})}
,
and prove that
{\langle{q^{\prime},{{q}^{\#}}^{\prime}}\rangle}\in R
.
We omit the justification of conditions for which all relevant values are unchanged between the pre-state and post-state.
In particular:

•

If
a\notin\mathsf{R}
then from
L
being response-activated we have
L(e\cdot t)=L(e)
,
and so conditions for which no other values are changed are omitted.

•

For conditions on each
p\in\mathsf{Tid}
, we justify them only for thread ids for which the relevant values were changed.
Specifically we note that
i^{e}_{p}=i^{e\cdot t}_{p}
whenever
t
is not an invocation or response of thread
p
, and in such cases as
\mathsf{h}({e})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})
we also have
i^{e}_{p}\in\mathsf{h}({e})\implies i^{e\cdot t}_{p}\in\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})

We divide into cases based on
a
:

•

If
a\in\mathsf{I}
, then it is of the form
a=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathit{m},v_{\text{in}},p,k}\rangle}
.
From
e\cdot t
being a well formed execution we get that
k
does not appear in any action in
\mathsf{h}({e})|_{p}
and thus from
Item

3
we have
k\notin ID(p)
.
We also obtain that necessarily
i^{e}_{p}=\varepsilon
and thus from
Item

66a
we have
\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{main}
.

–

If
\mathit{m}=\mathtt{read}
, then
v_{\text{in}}=\bot
and a transition using
read-inv
is available:

{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}_{\mathtt{DR}}}{}{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k],\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{r_{b}}],\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}
.

Then, as
\ell=0
, a transition using
read-init
is available:

{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k],\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{r_{b}}],\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}_{\mathtt{DR}}}{}\\
{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k],\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{r_{l}}],\mathit{VS}[p\mapsto\{{v}\}],\mathit{S}[p\mapsto\mathit{Ver}],\mathit{A}}\rangle}
.

For
Item

3
, note that
{\mathtt{id}}(a)=k\in ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k](p)
and indeed
a\in\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})|_{p}
.
For
Item

4
we use the fact that
\mathit{S}[p\mapsto\mathit{Ver}](p)=\mathit{Ver}
.
For
Item

5
we note that
i^{e\cdot t}_{p}=a
,
and thus

\mathtt{pref}(i^{e\cdot t}_{p})=\mathtt{pref}_{e\cdot t}({t})=e
, and we obtain that

\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e\cdot t}_{p}))|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
, as required.
For
Item

6
, we show
Item

66b
holds. We have
\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{r_{l}}](p)=\mathtt{r_{l}}
. As
i^{e\cdot t}_{p}=a
, we have that
i^{e\cdot t}_{p}=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{read},\bot,p,k}\rangle}
, where as required
k=ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k](p)[\mathsf{last}]
.
Moreover,
i^{e\cdot t}_{p}=a\in\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})
, and from
L(e)
being a valid linearization
i^{e\cdot t}_{p}\notin L(e)=L(e\cdot t)
.
Finally, we need to show for
Item

66b
that

\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e\cdot t}_{p})),L({e\cdot t}))\subseteq\mathit{VS}[p\mapsto\{{v}\}](p)
.

As
\mathtt{pref}(i^{e\cdot t}_{p})=e
and
L(e)=L(e\cdot t)

we have that
\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e\cdot t}_{p})),L({e\cdot t}))=\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(e),L(e))
.
As there is no
w\in L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}}
such that
w^{\prime}<_{L(e)}w
for all
w^{\prime}\in L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}}
,
we obtain that
\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(e),L(e))=\{{\mathtt{obs}(L(e))}\}
.
Overall the requirement simplifies to

\{{\mathtt{obs}(L(e))}\}\subseteq\{{v}\}
, which follows from
Item

1
.

–

If
\mathit{m}=\mathtt{write}
, then a transition using
write-inv
is available:

{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}_{\mathtt{DR}}}{}\\
{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k],\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{b}}],\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}[p\mapsto v_{\text{in}}]}\rangle}
.

Then, as
\ell=0
, a transition using
write-init
is available:

{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k],\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{b}}],\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}[p\mapsto v_{\text{in}}]}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p}\rangle}}$}_{\mathtt{DR}}}{}\\
{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k],\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{w}}],\mathit{VS},\mathit{S}[p\mapsto\mathit{Ver}],\mathit{A}[p\mapsto v_{\text{in}}]}\rangle}
.

Conditions
Item

3
,
Item

4
, and
Item

5
are justified exactly like in the case of
\mathit{m}=\mathtt{read}
.
For
Item

6
, we show
Item

66c
holds. We have
\mathit{PC}[p\mapsto\mathtt{w_{w}}](p)=\mathtt{w_{w}}
. As explained in the case of
\mathit{m}=\mathtt{read}
,
i^{e\cdot t}_{p}=a
. Here this implies that
i^{e\cdot t}_{p}=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{write},v_{\text{in}},p,k}\rangle}
, where as required
k=ID[p\mapsto ID(p)\cdot k](p)[\mathsf{last}]
and
v_{\text{in}}=\mathit{A}[p\mapsto v_{\text{in}}](p)
.
The fact that
i^{e\cdot t}_{p}\in\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})
and
i^{e\cdot t}_{p}\notin L(e\cdot t)
is justified exactly like in the case of
\mathit{m}=\mathtt{read}
.

•

If
a\in\mathsf{IInt}
, we perform an empty sequence of transitions.
Note that
\mathsf{h}({e})=\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})
,
and more generally no values relevant for the conditions are changed, so all conditions are maintained.

•

If
a\in\mathsf{R}
, it is of the form
a=\mathtt{res}{\langle{\mathit{m},v_{\text{out}},p,k}\rangle}
.
As
\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})
is well formed, this response corresponds to an invocation. That is,
i^{e}_{p}\neq\varepsilon
and
o\triangleq i^{e}_{p}\cdot a
is an inv-res pair (i.e., an operation).
Specifically
p={\mathtt{tid}}(i^{e}_{p})
,
k={\mathtt{id}}(i^{e}_{p})
, and from
Item

6
we get
k=ID(p)[\mathsf{last}]
.

The sequence of transitions we perform will be composed of two parts:

1.

A sequence of write and read transitions, with a structure that depends on whether a new write was linearized to the right of all existing writes (i.e.,
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
).

2.

A sequence with a response transition matching
a
.

For the first part, we begin by proving two technical claims about transitions available for particular sets of thread identifiers.

Denote
W=\mathsf{set}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\setminus\mathsf{set}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})

the set of writes which appear in the linearization of
e\cdot t
but not in the linearization of
e
.
Also denote
T_{W}=\{{{\mathtt{tid}}(w)\;|\;w\in W}\}
.

Claim 54.1:

Let
p^{\prime}\in T_{W}
. The following holds:

1.

\mathit{PC}(p^{\prime})=\mathtt{w_{w}}
,
i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{write},\mathit{A}(p^{\prime}),p^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\rangle}
where
k^{\prime}=ID(p^{\prime})[\mathsf{last}]
,
and
\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})={\mathtt{input}}(w^{\prime})
for a unique write operation
w^{\prime}\in W
such that
{\mathtt{tid}}(w^{\prime})=p^{\prime}
.

2.

\{{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})\;|\;p^{\prime}\in T_{W}}\}=\{{{\mathtt{input}}(w^{\prime})\;|\;w^{\prime}\in W}\}
.

3.

If additionally
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
, then
\mathit{S}(p^{\prime})<\mathit{Ver}
.

The idea here is that writes that were just added to the linearization following the last transition are all pending writes which did not store their value yet before said transition.

{claimproof}

Let
w^{\prime}\in W
be some write operation such that
{\mathtt{tid}}(w^{\prime})=p^{\prime}
. We prove all required properties:

1.

Let
i_{w^{\prime}}
be the invocation of
w^{\prime}
.
As
w^{\prime}\in\mathsf{set}(L(e\cdot t))
, by definition of linearizability it holds that
i_{w^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})
.
As
a=\mathsf{label}(t)\in\mathsf{R}
we have that
i_{w^{\prime}}\neq a
and so
i_{w^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})=\mathsf{h}({e})\cdot a
implies that
i_{w^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{h}({e})
.
As
w^{\prime}\notin\mathsf{set}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})

we obtain that
i_{w^{\prime}}=i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}
as the invocation must be pending in
e
to not appear in the linearization
L(e)
.
This also means
w^{\prime}
is a unique operation in
W
such that
{\mathtt{tid}}(w^{\prime})=p^{\prime}
, as no thread has two pending operations at once.
Overall we have
i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{h}({e})
,
i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}\notin L(e)
and
{\mathtt{method}}(i^{e}_{p^{\prime}})=\mathtt{write}
and thus
Item

66c
must hold and we obtain that
\mathit{PC}(p^{\prime})=\mathtt{w_{w}}
and
i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{write},\mathit{A}(p^{\prime}),p^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\rangle}
.
In particular as
i_{w^{\prime}}=i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}=\mathtt{inv}{\langle{\mathtt{write},\mathit{A}(p^{\prime}),p^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\rangle}
we obtain that
\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})={\mathtt{input}}(w^{\prime})
.

2.

As every
w^{\prime}\in W
has a corresponding
{\mathtt{tid}}(w^{\prime})\in T_{W}
, and as we have shown
\mathit{A}({\mathtt{tid}}(w^{\prime}))={\mathtt{input}}(w^{\prime})
,
we overall obtain that

\{{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})\;|\;p^{\prime}\in T_{W}}\}=\{{{\mathtt{input}}(w^{\prime})\;|\;w^{\prime}\in W}\}
.

3.

Assume additionally
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
.
Using
Item

2
we have
\mathit{S}(p^{\prime})\leq\mathit{Ver}
and thus it suffices to show that
\mathit{S}(p^{\prime})\neq\mathit{Ver}
.

Assume otherwise.
As
w^{\prime}\in\mathsf{set}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})
we have in particular that
L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}}\neq\varepsilon
and so
w^{\mathsf{last}}\triangleq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\varepsilon
.
Using
Item

5
and
\mathit{S}(p^{\prime})=\mathit{Ver}

we obtain that

\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}))|_{\mathtt{write}})
.
As we assume
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
,
we overall obtain that
w^{\mathsf{last}}=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}))|_{\mathtt{write}})
.
Now, denote
o^{\mathsf{last}}\triangleq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p^{\prime}})))

we obtain that

w^{\mathsf{last}}\leq_{L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}))}o^{\mathsf{last}}
.
Using decisiveness of
L
we have that
L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}))\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}L(e\cdot t)
.
We deduce that
w^{\mathsf{last}}\leq_{L(e\cdot t)}o^{\mathsf{last}}
.
From
L
being last-completed we also have that
o^{\mathsf{last}}\in\mathsf{set}(\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p^{\prime}})})))
.
That is, the response of
o^{\mathsf{last}}
appears in
e
before the invocation of
w^{\prime}
.
This implies
o^{\mathsf{last}}<_{\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})}w^{\prime}
and thus
o^{\mathsf{last}}<_{L(e\cdot t)}w^{\prime}
.
Overall
w^{\mathsf{last}}<_{L(e\cdot t)}w^{\prime}
, contradicting the definition of
w^{\mathsf{last}}
.
\claimqedhere

Denote
T_{R}=\{{\hat{p}\in\mathsf{Tid}\;|\;\mathit{PC}(\hat{p})=\mathtt{r_{l}}}\}
the set of thread identifiers of pending read operations currently executing their loop.

Claim 54.2:

\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}})),L(e\cdot t))\subseteq\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})\cup\{{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})\;|\;p^{\prime}\in T_{W}}\}

for every
\hat{p}\in T_{R}
.
If additionally
\mathit{S}(\hat{p})=\mathit{Ver}
and
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
, then

\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}})),L(e\cdot t))\subseteq\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})
.

The idea here is that any potential return value for a pending read is either already present in the set of values loaded by that read, or is the input value of some write which was just added to the linearization.
The second option is not needed if the read method started on the latest version number,
and the added writes were inserted between existing writes, as those are essentially performed with an earlier version numbers.

{claimproof}

Let
v^{\prime}\in\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}})),L(e\cdot t))
.
Using the definition of
\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}
, there are two cases to consider:

–

If
v^{\prime}=\mathtt{obs}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}})))
:

By definition
v^{\prime}\in\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}})),L(e))
.
Since
\mathit{PC}(\hat{p})=\mathtt{r_{l}}
we obtain from
Item

66b
that
\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}})),L(e))\subseteq\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})
,
and thus
v^{\prime}\in\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})
.

–

If
v^{\prime}={\mathtt{input}}(w^{\prime})
for some
w^{\prime}\in L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}}

such that
\hat{w}<_{L(e\cdot t)}w^{\prime}
for all
\hat{w}\in L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}}))|_{\mathtt{write}}
:

We consider two sub-cases:

*

If
w^{\prime}\notin W=\mathsf{set}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\setminus\mathsf{set}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
:

As
w^{\prime}\in L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}}
, it must be the case that
w^{\prime}\in L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}}
.
Since
L
is decisive we have
L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}}))\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}L(e)\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}L(e\cdot t)
.
Therefore, for every
\hat{w}\in L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}}))
,

\hat{w}\in L(e)
and
\hat{w}<_{L(e\cdot t)}w^{\prime}
implies that
\hat{w}<_{L(e)}w^{\prime}
.
Overall by definition of
\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}
we obtain that
v^{\prime}={\mathtt{input}}(w^{\prime})\in\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}})),L(e))
,
which as before implies that
v^{\prime}\in\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})
.

*

If
w^{\prime}\in W
:

By definition
v^{\prime}\in\{{{\mathtt{input}}(w)\;|\;w\in W}\}
.
Using
•
‣
Section

F.3
we have

\{{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})\;|\;p^{\prime}\in T_{W}}\}=\{{{\mathtt{input}}(w)\;|\;w\in W}\}
,
and so
v^{\prime}\in\{{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})\;|\;p^{\prime}\in T_{W}}\}
.

Assume now additionally that
\mathit{S}(\hat{p})=\mathit{Ver}
and
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
.
It suffices to show that the case
w^{\prime}\in W
is now impossible.
(Indeed, in all other cases we proved that
v^{\prime}\in\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})
.)
Assume otherwise.
From
\mathit{S}(\hat{p})=\mathit{Ver}
, using
Item

5
we deduce that
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}}))|_{\mathtt{write}})
.
Thus using the assumption that
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})

we overall obtain that
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}}))|_{\mathtt{write}})
.
Since
w^{\prime}\in L(e\cdot t)
we have that

w^{\mathsf{last}}\triangleq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\varepsilon
,
and as
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p}))|_{\mathtt{write}})

we deduce in particular that
w^{\mathsf{last}}\in L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p}))|_{\mathtt{write}}

By choice of
w^{\prime}
this implies
w^{\mathsf{last}}<_{L(e\cdot t)}w^{\prime}
,
contradicting the definition of
w^{\mathsf{last}}
.

We use the following claim to capture all relevant properties of the first part of the sequence we construct.
This then enables us to argue about the second part and the satisfaction of the required conditions in the post-states in a way which captures both cases for the first part at once.

Claim 54.3:

There exists a sequence of transitions
{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}\Longrightarrow^{*}\\
{\langle{v^{\prime},\mathit{Ver}^{\prime},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}[\forall p^{\prime}\in T_{W}\ldotp p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}],\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}

such that the following holds:

1.

v^{\prime}=\mathtt{obs}(L(e\cdot t))
.

2.

\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}\geq\mathit{Ver}
.

3.

\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}>\mathit{Ver}\iff\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
.

4.

\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}})),L(e\cdot t))\subseteq\mathit{VS}^{\prime}(\hat{p})
for all
\hat{p}\in T_{R}
.

{claimproof}

Denote
w^{\mathsf{last}}\triangleq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})
.
Consider the following cases:

–

If
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
:

First, we claim that
w^{\mathsf{last}}\neq\varepsilon
and
p^{\mathsf{last}}\triangleq{\mathtt{tid}}(w^{\mathsf{last}})\in T_{W}
.

Indeed,
L(e)\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}L(e\cdot t)
due to
L
being decisive, and from
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
necessarily
L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}}\precneqq_{\mathsf{S}}L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}}
and thus
w^{\mathsf{last}}=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\varepsilon
.

Moreover, it is not possible that
w^{\mathsf{last}}\in L(e)
because then we would get from
L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}}\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}}
that
w^{\mathsf{last}}=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
, contradicting
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
.
Therefore, we obtain that
w^{\mathsf{last}}\in W
and
p^{\mathsf{last}}\in T_{W}
.

Now, from
Item

2
we have
\ell=0
, and as we have shown in
•
‣
Section

F.3
that
\mathit{PC}(p^{\prime})=\mathtt{w_{w}}
for all
p^{\prime}\in T_{W}
, a transition using the rule
write-top
is available for them.

Using
Item

4
, a transition using
read-loop
is available for each
\hat{p}\in T_{R}
where the current value in the register is not in its set of loaded values already.
As
\mathit{Ver}
only increases and
\mathit{S}(\hat{p})
is not changed by
write-top
or
read-loop
transitions,
read-loop
remains available after performing these transitions (again dependent on the current register value).

Overall, we can perform a
write-top
transition for every
p^{\prime}\in T_{W}
, doing the transition for
p^{\mathsf{last}}
last, where each such transition is followed by a
read-loop
transition for every
\hat{p}\in T_{R}
that does not already contain the value stored in the
write-top
transition.
After performing this sequence, for every
\hat{p}\in T_{R}
, every value in
\{{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})\;|\;p^{\prime}\in T_{W}}\}
is either added to
\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})
or is already present in that set.
This overall gives:

{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}\Longrightarrow^{*}\\
{\langle{\mathit{A}(p^{\mathsf{last}}),\mathit{Ver}+\left|{T_{W}}\right|,\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}[\forall p^{\prime}\in T_{W}\ldotp p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}],\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}

where
\mathit{VS}^{\prime}=\mathit{VS}[\forall\hat{p}\in T_{R}\ldotp\hat{p}\mapsto\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})\cup\{{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})\;|\;p^{\prime}\in T_{W}}\}]
.
We show all conditions required for the claim hold:

1.

\mathit{A}(p^{\mathsf{last}})=\mathtt{obs}(L(e\cdot t))
:

As we defined
p^{\mathsf{last}}={\mathtt{tid}}(w^{\mathsf{last}})
where
w^{\mathsf{last}}=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})
, we have
\mathtt{obs}(L(e\cdot t))={\mathtt{input}}(w^{\mathsf{last}})
.
As we have shown
p^{\mathsf{last}}\in T_{W}
,
we obtain from
•
‣
Section

F.3
that
\mathit{A}(p^{\mathsf{last}})={\mathtt{input}}(w^{\mathsf{last}})
, and the equality
\mathit{A}(p^{\mathsf{last}})=\mathtt{obs}(L(e\cdot t))
follows.

2.

\mathit{Ver}+\left|{T_{W}}\right|\geq\mathit{Ver}
:

Immediate from
\left|{T_{W}}\right|\geq 0
.

3.

\mathit{Ver}+\left|{T_{W}}\right|>\mathit{Ver}\iff\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
:

We have shown
p^{\mathsf{last}}\in T_{W}
.
This implies
\left|{T_{W}}\right|>0
and so the LHS is true.
The RHS is true by assumption.

4.

\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}})),L(e\cdot t))\subseteq\mathit{VS}^{\prime}(\hat{p})
for all
\hat{p}\in T_{R}
:

Let
\hat{p}\in T_{R}
. Using
•
‣
Section

F.3
we have that

\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{\hat{p}})),L(e\cdot t))\subseteq\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})\cup\{{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})\;|\;p^{\prime}\in T_{W}}\}=\mathit{VS}^{\prime}(\hat{p})
.

–

If
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
:

Using
•
‣
Section

F.3
we have all neccessary conditions to perform a
write-roll-back
transition for every
p^{\prime}\in T_{W}
.
(In particular,
\mathit{S}(p^{\prime})<\mathit{Ver}
holds since we assume
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})

Doing this transition for a given

gives

{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{\mathtt{int}{\langle{p^{\prime}}\rangle}}$}}{}\\
{\langle{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime}),\mathit{Ver}-1,1,ID,\mathit{PC}[p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{r}}],\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}[p^{\prime}\mapsto v]}\rangle}

Define
T_{R}^{\prime}\triangleq\{{\hat{p}\;|\;\mathit{PC}(\hat{p})=\mathtt{r_{l}}\land\mathit{S}(\hat{p})\leq\mathit{Ver}-1}\}
.
After the above transition, a
read-loop
transition can be done for each
\hat{p}\in T_{R}^{\prime}
for whom the set of loaded values does not contain the current register value,
and this remains true when performing other

Preforming such a transition for each relevant
\hat{p}\in T_{R}^{\prime}
gives:

{\langle{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime}),\mathit{Ver}-1,1,ID,\mathit{PC}[p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{r}}],\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}[p^{\prime}\mapsto v]}\rangle}\Longrightarrow^{*}\\
{\langle{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime}),\mathit{Ver}-1,1,ID,\mathit{PC}[p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{r}}],\tilde{\mathit{VS}},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}[p^{\prime}\mapsto v]}\rangle}

Where

\tilde{\mathit{VS}}=\mathit{VS}[\forall\hat{p}\in T_{R}^{\prime}\ldotp\hat{p}\mapsto\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})\cup\{{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})}\}]

After that, a

{\langle{\mathit{A}[p^{\prime}\mapsto v](p^{\prime}),\mathit{Ver}-1+1,0,ID,\mathit{PC}[p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}],\tilde{\mathit{VS}},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}[p^{\prime}\mapsto v]}\rangle}

Simplifying this (using
\ell=0
) we obtain a state

{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}[p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}],\tilde{\mathit{VS}},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}[p^{\prime}\mapsto v]}\rangle}

After such a sequence, a similar sequence for other

Overall, we can perform this sequence of each

, and get:

{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC},\mathit{VS},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}}\rangle}\Longrightarrow^{*}\\
{\langle{v,\mathit{Ver},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}[\forall p^{\prime}\in T_{W}\ldotp p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}],\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}[\forall p^{\prime}\in T_{W}\ldotp p^{\prime}\mapsto v]}\rangle}

Where

\mathit{VS}^{\prime}=\mathit{VS}[\forall\hat{p}\in T_{R}^{\prime}\ldotp\hat{p}\mapsto\mathit{VS}(\hat{p})\cup\{{\mathit{A}(p^{\prime})\;|\;p^{\prime}\in T_{W}}\}]
.
Note that it is possible that
T_{W}=\emptyset
, and in this case the above sequence is empty and the post-state is equal to the pre-state.

We show all conditions required for the claim hold:

1.

v=\mathtt{obs}(L(e\cdot t))
:

This follows from

holding in the pre-state and the fact that
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})

and thus

2.

\mathit{Ver}\geq\mathit{Ver}
:

Immediate.

3.

\mathit{Ver}>\mathit{Ver}\iff\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
:

The RHS is false by assumption and thus this statement is true.

4.

\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p})),L(e\cdot t))\subseteq\mathit{VS}^{\prime}(p)
for all
\hat{p}\in T_{R}
:

*

If

As

we can use

and obtain that

\claimqedhere

We move on to argue about the second part of the sequence where we perform a sequence ending with a response labeled

.
The structure of this sequence depends on

.

Claim 54.4:

Starting from the post-state described in

{\langle{v^{\prime},\mathit{Ver}^{\prime},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}[\forall p^{\prime}\in T_{W}\ldotp p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}][p\mapsto\mathtt{main}],\mathit{VS}^{\prime}[p\mapsto\emptyset],\mathit{S}[p\mapsto 0],\mathit{A}^{\prime}[p\mapsto 0]}\rangle}

{claimproof}

As

–

must hold and thus

,

, and from

{\langle{v^{\prime},\mathit{Ver}^{\prime},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}[\forall p^{\prime}\in T_{W}\ldotp p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}][p\mapsto\mathtt{main}],\mathit{VS}^{\prime}[p\mapsto\emptyset],\mathit{S}[p\mapsto 0],\mathit{A}^{\prime}[p\mapsto 0]}\rangle}

–

If
\mathit{m}=\mathtt{write}

We have two options:
The first option is that

, in which case

66d
must hold, and we get that

.

, we also get that

and so

.

. As

and thus

and we deduce

.

with label

is available:

{\langle{v^{\prime},\mathit{Ver}^{\prime},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}[\forall p^{\prime}\in T_{W}\ldotp p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}],\mathit{VS}^{\prime},\mathit{S},\mathit{A}^{\prime}}\rangle}{}\mathrel{\raisebox{-0.8pt}{$\xrightarrow{a}$}_{\mathtt{DR}}}{}\\
{\langle{v^{\prime},\mathit{Ver}^{\prime},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}[\forall p^{\prime}\in T_{W}\ldotp p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}][p\mapsto\mathtt{main}],\mathit{VS}^{\prime}[p\mapsto\emptyset],\mathit{S}[p\mapsto 0],\mathit{A}^{\prime}[p\mapsto 0]}\rangle}
.
\claimqedhere

Using
•
‣
Sections

F.3
and
•
‣
F.3
, we reach a state of the form

{\langle{v^{\prime},\mathit{Ver}^{\prime},\ell,ID,\mathit{PC}^{\prime},\mathit{VS}^{\prime}[p\mapsto\emptyset],\mathit{S}[p\mapsto 0],\mathit{A}^{\prime}[p\mapsto 0]}\rangle}

where
\mathit{PC}^{\prime}=\mathit{PC}[\forall p^{\prime}\in T_{W}\ldotp p^{\prime}\mapsto\mathtt{w_{e}}][p\mapsto\mathtt{main}]
,
such that:

1.

.

2.

\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}\geq\mathit{Ver}
.

3.

\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}>\mathit{Ver}\iff\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})\neq\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})

4.

\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p})),L(e\cdot t))\subseteq\mathit{VS}^{\prime}(p)
for all
\hat{p}\in T_{R}
.

We show that all necessary conditions are satisfied.
For
Item

1
, we have that indeed
v^{\prime}=\mathtt{obs}(L(e\cdot t))
.
For
Item

4
we use the fact that
Item

4
holds in the pre-states:
We get that
\mathit{S}[p\mapsto 0](p)=0\leq\mathit{Ver}\leq\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}
,
and for all

we have
\mathit{S}[p\mapsto 0](p^{\prime})=\mathit{S}(p^{\prime})\leq\mathit{Ver}\leq\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}
.
For
Item

5
, consider two cases:

–

If
\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}>\mathit{Ver}
:

Using the fact that

for all
p^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Tid}
, the condition
\mathit{S}[p\mapsto 0](p^{\prime})=\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}
is always false and
Item

5
holds vacuously.

–

If
\mathit{Ver}^{\prime}=\mathit{Ver}
:

We deduce that
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
.

For
p^{\prime}\neq p
,
i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}=i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}
and so overall no values relevant for
Item

5
have changed.
For

we have

and thus

and the condition follows.

For
Item

6
, let
p^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Tid}
.
Consider the following cases:

–

If
p^{\prime}\neq p
and
\mathit{PC}^{\prime}(p)=\mathtt{main}
:

We deduce that
p^{\prime}\notin T_{W}
and so
\mathit{PC}(p)=\mathtt{main}
and
Item

66a
holds in the pre-states. As
i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}=i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}=\varepsilon
,
Item

66a
continues to hold in the post-states.

–

If
p^{\prime}=p
:

We have
\mathit{PC}^{\prime}(p)=\mathtt{main}
and as
o
is completed and
e\cdot t
is well formed we deduce that
i^{e\cdot t}_{p}=\varepsilon
and
Item

66a
holds.

–

If
p^{\prime}\in T_{R}\setminus\{{p}\}
and
\mathit{PC}^{\prime}(p^{\prime})=\mathtt{r_{l}}
:

As
p^{\prime}\neq p
and
\mathit{PC}(p^{\prime})=\mathtt{r_{l}}
, using
Item

66b
we have
i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}=i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{h}({e})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t})
.
To show
i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}\notin L(e\cdot t)
, using the fact that
L
is pending-read-free it is sufficient to prove that
i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}\notin\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e\cdot t}))
.
Indeed, if we assume otherwise, as
t
is the response for
p\neq p^{\prime}
we get
i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e}))
.
Since
Item

66b
holds in the pre-state we have
i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}\notin L(e)
, and overall this contradicts
L
being a linearization mapping.
We get that
i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}\notin L(e\cdot t)
.
Additionally, since
p^{\prime}\in T_{R}
we have shown in
•
‣
Section

F.3
that
\mathtt{obs\text{-}between}(L(\mathtt{pref}(i^{e}_{p^{\prime}})),L(e\cdot t))\subseteq\mathit{VS}^{\prime}(p^{\prime})
.
As
i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}=i^{e}_{p^{\prime}}
and
\mathit{VS}^{\prime}[p\mapsto\emptyset](p^{\prime})=\mathit{VS}^{\prime}(p^{\prime})
, we deduce that the condition on
\mathit{VS}
holds and overall
Item

66b
holds.

–

If
p^{\prime}\in T_{W}
:

We have
\mathit{PC}^{\prime}(p^{\prime})=\mathtt{w_{e}}
and by definition of
T_{W}
we obtain that
w\in L(e\cdot t)
for the write operation
w\in W
such that
{\mathtt{tid}}(w)=p^{\prime}
. (It is unique due to
•
‣
Section

F.3
.)
As
i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}
is the invocation of
w
we get
i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}\in L(e\cdot t)
and
{\mathtt{method}}(i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}})=\mathtt{write}
, and so
Item

66d
holds.

–

Otherwise:

It must be the case that
i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}
is a write invocation and
p^{\prime}\notin T_{W}
.
By definition of
T_{W}
this means
i^{e\cdot t}_{p^{\prime}}\notin L(e\cdot t)
and overall we get
Item

66c
from this condition holding in the pre-states.∎

Theorem 55
.

\mathtt{WSR}
is
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
-hard.

Proof Sketch.

The proof follows along very similar lines to
Thm.

54
.
The simulation relation is the same, except for the omission of
Item

2
,
Item

4
, and
Item

5
as they are irrelevant to the state of
\mathtt{WSR}
.
For a write strong implementation, we get via
Thm.

50
a linearization mapping
L
that is write strong in addition to being lazy.

Using the fact that
L
is write strong, in the second case considered in the proof of
•
‣
Section

F.3
(
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
),
we can deduce that
L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}}=L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}}
.
That is, no new writes are added to the linearization following the transition
t
, and so
T_{W}=\emptyset
.
As the “rollback” mechanism of
\mathtt{DR}
is only used to construct a sequence in the proof of
•
‣
Section

F.3
under an assumption that
\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e\cdot t)|_{\mathtt{write}})=\mathsf{last_{op}}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}})
and
T_{W}\neq\emptyset
,
and without using it the mechanism involving version numbers is redundant, as they can only increase,
we overall have that the mechanisms in
\mathtt{DR}
that are not available in
\mathtt{WSR}
are not needed for the simulation, and we are able to use
\mathtt{WSR}
to simulate the implementation.
∎

Appendix G
Lazy Linearizations

In this section we prove the existence of linearizations with the properties described in
Theorems

50
and
51
.

In
Section

G.1
we discuss the existence and usefulness of linearization mappings that are minimal w.r.t. preorders satisfying certain properties.
In
Section

G.2
/
Section

G.3
we prove several properties of write strong/decisive linearizations that are minimal w.r.t. certain preorders.
In
Section

G.4
we use the above to prove
Theorems

50
and
51
.

G.1
Minimal Linearization Mappings

In this section we describe the general framework we use to construct linearization mappings with desirable properties. This framework is not specific to register objects.
The construction is generally done via the following stages:

1.

Define a notion of minimality among linearization mapping that satisfy a certain condition. (e.g., decisive linearization mappings.) Intuitively, the notions of minimality we use represent “delaying as much as possible decision on ordering between operations”, hence minimal mappings are also called “lazy”.

2.

Prove that a minimal mapping exists.

3.

Prove that minimal mappings have desirable properties. (In the write strong case we also show they can be modified to obtain additional properties not guaranteed by all minimal mappings.)

We begin by formally defining notions related to preorders and equivalences:

Preorders.

A preorder
\precsim
on a set
A
is a reflexive and transitive relation over
A
.
An element
x\in A
is
\precsim
-minimal

if
y\precsim x
implies
x\precsim y
for every
y\in A
.

\precsim
is
well-founded
if every non-empty
B\subseteq A
has a
\precsim
-minimal element.
A
\precsim
-chain
is a subset
C\subseteq A
such that
\precsim
is a total order (not just preorder)
on
C
.
An element
x\in A
is a
\precsim
-lower bound
of a set
B\subseteq A
if
x\precsim y
for all
y\in B
.
If we also have
x\in B
, we say that
x
is a (not necessarily unique)
\precsim
-minimum
of
B
.

To find minima we use the following classical lemma by Zorn (stated here for preorders and minima):

Lemma 56

(Zorn)
.

If
\precsim
is a preorder on a set
A

and every
\precsim
-chain
C\subseteq A
has a
\precsim
-lower bound,
then
A
contains a
\precsim
-minimal element.

Equivalences.

An equivalence
\sim
on a set
A
is a reflexive, transitive and symmetric relation over
A
.

\sim
is a
congruence
w.r.t. a relation
R\subseteq A\times A
if
{\langle{a,c}\rangle}\in R\iff{\langle{b,d}\rangle}\in R
whenever
a\sim b
and
c\sim d
.

In the sequel, we use
\precsim
to denote an arbitrary preorder, and
{\sim}\triangleq{\precsim}\,\cap\,{\precsim^{-1}}
to denote the equivalence relation induced by
\precsim
.

Due to transitivity, a preorder and the induced equivalence satisfy the following:

Lemma 57
.

Let
\precsim
be a preorder and let
{\sim}\triangleq{\precsim}\,\cap\,{\precsim^{-1}}
be the induced equivalence.
Then,

\sim
is a
congruence
w.r.t.
\precsim
.

Preorders on specifications are lifted point-wise to preorders on linearization mappings:

Notation 58
.

Given an implementation
I
, a specification
\mathit{Spec}
,
linearization mappings
L_{1},L_{2}:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}
,
and a preorder
\precsim
on
\mathit{Spec}
,
we write
L_{1}\precsim L_{2}

if
L_{1}(e)\precsim L_{2}(e)
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
.

Note that if
\precsim
is a preorder on specifications,
then its point-wise lift is a preorder on linearization mappings.

We are interested in families of linearization mappings for a specific implementation that satisfy a condition on the linearizations of prefixes.
(Strong, write strong and decisive linearizability are all instances of such a definition.)
This is formalized as follows:

Definition 59
.

Let
I
be an implementation of an object
O
and let
\mathit{Spec}
be a specification of
O
.
A linearization mapping
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}

respects
a relation
R\subseteq\mathit{Spec}\times\mathit{Spec}
if
{\langle{L(e_{1}),L(e_{2})}\rangle}\in R
for every

e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I})
such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.

Let
\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
denote the set of all linearization mappings
L:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}
that respect
R
.
The following lemma is used to prove the existence of linearization mappings that are
\precsim
-minimal in
\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
:

Lemma 60
.

Let
I
be an implementation.
Let
\precsim
be a well-founded preorder on a specification
\mathit{Spec}
such that
\sim\triangleq{\precsim}\,\cap\,{\precsim^{-1}}
is a congruence w.r.t. a relation
R\subseteq\mathit{Spec}\times\mathit{Spec}
.
If
\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)

contains some
\precsim
-minimal linearization mapping.

Note that while
\precsim
is assumed to be well-founded,
this property is not generally preserved when lifting point-wise to linearization mappings,
as they may be decreased infinitely often w.r.t.
\precsim
, by decreasing the linearizations of different executions.
Thus an additional argument is required to obtain a
\precsim
-minimal mapping.

Proof.

In the following proof,
to avoid confusion between
\precsim
and its point-wise lifting to linearization mappings, we will use
\precsim^{*}
to denote the point-wise lifting.

Using Zorn’s Lemma, it is sufficient to prove that any
\precsim^{*}
-chain of linearization mappings in
\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
has a
\precsim^{*}
-lower bound.

Let
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
be a
\precsim^{*}
-chain.

If
\mathscr{C}=\emptyset
, then any
L\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
is trivially a
\precsim^{*}
-lower bound of
\mathscr{C}
.

Otherwise, we define a
\precsim^{*}
-lower bound
L_{\text{bound}}:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}
as follows:

Let
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
.
Since
\precsim
is well-founded
and the set
\{{L(e)\;|\;L\in\mathscr{C}}\}

is non-empty,
it contains a (not necessarily unique)
\precsim
-minimal element.
Since
\precsim^{*}
is a total order on
\mathscr{C}
,
\precsim
is a total preorder on
\{{L(e)\;|\;L\in\mathscr{C}}\}

and so any such
\precsim
-minimal element is a
\precsim
-minimum.
We define
L_{\text{bound}}(e)
to be an arbitrarily chosen
\precsim
-minimum of
\{{L(e)\;|\;L\in\mathscr{C}}\}
.

Clearly,
L_{\text{bound}}
is a
\precsim^{*}
-lower bound on
\mathscr{C}
.

It remains to show that
L_{\text{bound}}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
.
It is immediate that it is a linearization mapping since for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
,

L_{\text{bound}}(e)=L^{\prime}(e)
for some
L^{\prime}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
.

Let
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I})
such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.
We show that
{\langle{L_{\text{bound}}(e_{1}),L_{\text{bound}}(e_{2})}\rangle}\in R
.

Let
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathscr{C}
be mappings such that
L_{\text{bound}}(e_{1})=L_{1}(e_{1})
and
L_{\text{bound}}(e_{2})=L_{2}(e_{2})
.

We show
{\langle{L_{1}(e_{1}),L_{2}(e_{2})}\rangle}\in R
.

Since
\precsim^{*}
is a total order on
\mathscr{C}
, one of the following holds:

•

L_{1}\precsim^{*}L_{2}
:

In this case, we have
L_{1}(e_{2})\precsim L_{2}(e_{2})
.
As
L_{1}\in\mathscr{C}
and
L_{\text{bound}}
is a
\precsim^{*}
-lower bound of
\mathscr{C}

we obtain that
L_{2}(e_{2})=L_{\text{bound}}(e_{2})\precsim L_{1}(e_{2})
.
Thus,
L_{1}(e_{2})\sim L_{2}(e_{2})
and from congruence of
\sim
w.r.t.
R
we obtain that

{\langle{L_{1}(e_{1}),L_{2}(e_{2})}\rangle}\in R\iff{\langle{L_{1}(e_{1}),L_{1}(e_{2})}\rangle}\in R
.
Then,
{\langle{L_{1}(e_{1}),L_{1}(e_{2})}\rangle}\in R
follows from the fact that
L_{1}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
.

•

L_{2}\precsim^{*}L_{1}
:
We similarly obtain that
L_{1}(e_{1})\sim L_{2}(e_{1})
,
and the desired property follows from
L_{2}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
.
∎

For (partial) orders, we obtain the following corollary:

Corollary 61
.

Let
I
be an implementation.
Let
\preceq
be a well-founded (partial) order on a specification
\mathit{Spec}
,
and let
R
be a relation on
\mathit{Spec}
.
If
\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
contains some
\preceq
-minimal linearization mapping.

Proof.

Follows from
Lemma

60
, using the fact that
\preceq\cap\preceq^{-1}
is equality, which is a congruence w.r.t. any relation
R
.
∎

An immediate consequence of the definition of minimality is that one cannot strictly decrease sequential histories returned by minimal mappings that respect
R
without violating the respect of
R
.
In other words, any decrease which respects
R
is not a strict decrease.
This fact will be useful to establish properties of these sequential histories.

Lemma 62
.

Let
I
be an implementation of an object
O
,
let
\mathit{Spec}
be a specification of
O
,
let
\precsim
be a preorder on
\mathit{Spec}
,
and let
R
be a reflexive relation on
\mathit{Spec}
.
Suppose that
L\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
is
\precsim
-minimal,

e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
,
and
s
is a sequential history of
O
,
such that:

1.

s\in\mathit{Spec}
,

2.

\mathsf{h}({e})\sqsubseteq s
,

3.

s\precsim L(e)
,

4.

{\langle{L(e^{\prime}),s}\rangle}\in R
for all
e^{\prime}\in\mathsf{E}({I})
such that
e^{\prime}\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}e
, and

5.

{\langle{s,L(e^{\prime})}\rangle}\in R
for all
e^{\prime}\in\mathsf{E}({I})
such that
e\precneqq_{\mathsf{P}}e^{\prime}
.

Then,
s\sim L(e)
.

Proof.

Consider the linearization mapping
L^{\prime}:\mathsf{E}({I})\to\mathit{Spec}
defined by

L^{\prime}(e^{\prime})=\begin{cases}L(e^{\prime})&e^{\prime}\neq e\\
s&e^{\prime}=e\end{cases}

It is easy to verify that
L^{\prime}\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathit{Spec}}^{R}(I)
and
L^{\prime}\precsim L
.
Since
L
is
\precsim
-minimal, we deduce that
L^{\prime}\sim L

and in particular
s=L^{\prime}(e)\sim L(e)
.
∎

G.2
Minimal Linearizations for Write-Strong Registers

In this section we apply the framework described in
Section

G.1
to write strong-linearizability.
First, we notice write strong-linearizability is an instance of
Definition

59
.
Indeed, define:

\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}\triangleq\{{{\langle{s_{1},s_{2}}\rangle}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}\times\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}\;|\;s_{1}|_{\mathtt{write}}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}s_{2}|_{\mathtt{write}}}\}

Then
\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}}^{\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}}(I)
is the set of write strong-linearizations of the implementation
I
,
and the class of write strongly-linearizable register implementations satisfies:

I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}\iff\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}}^{\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}}(I)\neq\emptyset

This is a restatement of the definition from the body of the paper, using the extra notation we now have.

Now, we define a preorder on
\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
(which provides a preorder on write strong linearizations via point-wise lifting):

Definition 63
.

For
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
, we define
s_{1}\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}s_{2}
if
\mathsf{set}(s_{1})\subseteq\mathsf{set}(s_{2})
and
([\mathtt{write}]\mathbin{;}<_{s_{1}})\subseteq<_{s_{2}}
.

Note that
\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}
is a preorder.
We define
\sim^{\mathsf{w*}}\triangleq(\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}\cap{(\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}})}^{-1})
, the induced equivalence.
The condition
([\mathtt{write}]\mathbin{;}<_{s_{1}})\subseteq<_{s_{2}}
means that if a write operation appears before some other operation is
s_{1}
, than this is also the case in
s_{2}
.
The idea behind the condition
\mathsf{set}(s_{1})\subseteq\mathsf{set}(s_{2})
is that linearizations that are
\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}
-minimal do not linearize operations unless they must.
Indeed, if we can get a valid linearization
s_{1}
(respecting conditions on prefixes) by removing some operation from
s_{2}
,
then a linearization mapping using
s_{1}
instead of
s_{2}
will be strictly smaller since
\mathsf{set}(s_{1})\subseteq\mathsf{set}(s_{2})
(and
([\mathtt{write}]\mathbin{;}<_{s_{1}})\subseteq<_{s_{2}}
) but
\mathsf{set}(s_{2})\nsubseteq\mathsf{set}(s_{1})
.
The condition
([\mathtt{write}]\mathbin{;}<_{s_{1}})\subseteq<_{s_{2}}
will be later used to show that minimal linearization avoid in some cases the re-ordering of already linearized operations in the linearization of an extension of an execution.
This will be a useful step towards showing that minimal linearizations can be modified to become decisive,
in which case the ordering between operations is never changed once they are linearized.

The following follow immediately from definition:

Lemma 64
.

Let
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
be register sequential histories.
If
s_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}s_{2}
, then
s_{1}\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}s_{2}
.

Lemma 65
.

Let
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
be register sequential histories.
If
s_{1}\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}s_{2}
, then
s_{1}|_{\mathtt{write}}\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}s_{2}|_{\mathtt{write}}
.

Using
Lemma

60
we obtain the existence of write strong-linearizations that are
\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}
-minimal:

Lemma 66
.

If
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
,
then
\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}}^{\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}}(I)
contains a
\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}
-minimal linearization.

Proof.

By
Lemma

60
it suffices to show that
\sim^{\mathsf{w*}}
is a congruence w.r.t.
\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}
.
Indeed,
if
s_{i}\sim^{\mathsf{w*}}s^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\in\{{1,2}\}
,
then from
Lemma

65
we have
s_{i}|_{\mathtt{write}}=s^{\prime}_{i}|_{\mathtt{write}}
and thus
s_{1}\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}s_{2}\iff s_{1}|_{\mathtt{write}}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}s_{2}|_{\mathtt{write}}\iff s^{\prime}_{1}|_{\mathtt{write}}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}s^{\prime}_{2}|_{\mathtt{write}}\iff s^{\prime}_{1}\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}s^{\prime}_{2}
.
∎

We wish to use
Lemma

62
to argue about properties of these minimal linearizations.
Due to
Lemma

57
, conditions 4 and 5 in
Lemma

62
can be replaced with a requirement that
s|_{\mathtt{write}}=L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}}
.
(In other words, replacing a linearization with another one with the same sequence of writes maintains the respect of
\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}
between prefixes.)
This gives the following corollary of
Lemma

62
:

Lemma 67
.

If
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
,
L\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}}^{\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}}(I)
is
\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}
-minimal,
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
,
and
s
is a sequential history of
\mathsf{Reg}
,
such that:

1.

s\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
,

2.

\mathsf{h}({e})\sqsubseteq s
,

3.

s\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}L(e)
, and

4.

s|_{\mathtt{write}}=L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}}
.

Then,
s\sim^{\mathsf{w*}}L(e)
.

We now begin the process of proving several properties of
\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}
-minimal write strong linearizations.
First, we show that they contain no pending reads:

Lemma 68
.

If
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
and
L\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}}^{\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}}(I)
is
\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}
-minimal,
then
L
is pending-read-free.

Proof.

We need to show
\mathsf{set}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{read}})\subseteq\mathsf{set}(\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e})))
for every
e\in\mathsf{E}({I})
.

Denote
s=L(e)|_{\mathsf{set}(L(e))\setminus\left(\mathsf{set}(L(e)|_{\mathtt{read}})\setminus\mathsf{set}(\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e})))\right)}
the subsequence of
L(e)
where every pending read operation is removed.
It is sufficient to show that
s=L(e)
.
We apply
Lemma

67
to
e
and
s
.
We have:

1.

s\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
:
Follows from
Lemma

38
.

2.

\mathsf{h}({e})\sqsubseteq s
:
Follows from
Lemma

34
, using the fact that
\mathsf{h}({e})\sqsubseteq L(e)
.

3.

s\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}L(e)
:
Using
Lemma

64
it is sufficient to verify that
s\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}L(e)
, which follows immediately from the construction of
s
.

4.

s|_{\mathtt{write}}=L(e)|_{\mathtt{write}}
:
This is again immediate from the construction, as we only remove reads from the sequence.

Overall, we can apply
Lemma

67
and deduce that
s\sim^{\mathsf{w*}}L(e)
.
This implies that
\mathsf{set}(s)=\mathsf{set}(L(e))
and as
s\preceq_{\mathsf{S}}L(e)
we obtain
s=L(e)
.
∎

Next, we prove several properties related to maintaining operations and the ordering between them in the linearizations when extending an execution.
These properties are proven as steps in the way to obtaining a minimal write strong-linearization mapping that is also decisive, a property which is stronger than those discussed in the lemmas leading up to that point.

We start this process by showing that once operations appear in the minimal linearization of some execution, they remain in the linearization for all extensions:

Lemma 69
.

If
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
and
L\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}}^{\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}}(I)
is
\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}
-minimal,
then
\mathsf{set}(L(e_{1}))\subseteq\mathsf{set}(L(e_{2}))
for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I})
such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
o\in L(e_{1})
. We consider two cases:
If
o
is a write operation, then from
L
being write strong we have
o\in L(e_{1})|_{\mathtt{write}}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}}

and thus
o\in L(e_{2})
.
If
o
is a read operation, from
Lemma

68
we have
o\in\mathsf{set}(\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e_{1}})))\subseteq\mathsf{set}(\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e_{2}})))
, and by definition of linearizability we must get
o\in L(e_{2})
, as required.
∎

Now, we show that not only is the set of operations respected by extensions of an execution,
but also the ordering between a write operation and a read operation is maintained.
(This is also true for two write operations, as an immediate consequence of the mapping being write strong.)

Intuitively, there are two types of violations we must consider,
given two executions
e_{1},e_{2}
such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
,
a write operation
w
, and a read operation
r
:
The first is that
w\leq_{L(e_{1})}r
and
r\leq_{L(e_{2})}w
,
and the second is that
r\leq_{L(e_{1})}w
and
w\leq_{L(e_{2})}r
.

We wish to exploit the symmetry between arguments required to show both of these violations cannot occur,
and also handle the case where
w
does not appear in
L(e_{1})
,
but as we show is still guaranteed to appear after
r
in
L(e_{2})
.

This is done by comparing the sequences
s_{1}\triangleq L(e_{1})\oplus(L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}})
(concatenating write operations not preset in
L(e_{1})
to the end of
L(e_{1})
)
and
s_{2}\triangleq L(e_{2})
.
These sequences can be handled symmetrically, using the observation that they both have the same subsequence of write operations:

Observation 70
.

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
, let
L\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}}^{\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}}(I)
,
let
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I})
be executions such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
,
and define
s_{1}=L(e_{1})\oplus(L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}})
,
s_{2}=L(e_{2})
.
Then,

s_{1}|_{\mathtt{write}}=s_{2}|_{\mathtt{write}}
.

Proof.

Since
L
is write strong we have
L(e_{1})|_{\mathtt{write}}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}}
.

That is,
s_{2}|_{\mathtt{write}}=L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}}=L(e_{1})|_{\mathtt{write}}\cdot L(e_{2})|_{\mathsf{set}(L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}})\setminus\mathsf{set}(L(e_{1})|_{\mathtt{write}})}=L(e_{1})|_{\mathtt{write}}\oplus L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}}=(L(e_{1})\oplus L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}})|_{\mathtt{write}}=s_{1}|_{\mathtt{write}}

∎

Lemma 71
.

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{ws}}
and
L\in\mathscr{L}_{\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}}^{\precsim_{\mathsf{P}}^{\mathsf{ww}}}(I)
such that
L
is
\precsim^{\mathsf{w*}}
-minimal.
For all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{E}({I})
such that
e_{1}\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}e_{2}
,
let
s_{1}=L(e_{1})\oplus(L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}})
and
s_{2}=L(e_{2})
.
Then,

([\mathtt{write}]\mathbin{;}(<_{s_{i}}\cap<_{s_{\bar{i}}}^{-1}))=\emptyset

for
(i,\bar{i})\in\{{(1,2),(2,1)}\}
.

The idea is that a member of
([\mathtt{write}]\mathbin{;}(<_{s_{i}}\cap<_{s_{\bar{i}}}^{-1}))
for some
i
is a pair of operations
{\langle{w,r}\rangle}
such that the sequences
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
disagree on the ordering between them,

w
is a write operation, and
r
must be a read operation (due to
L
being write strong).
This would be exactly a violation of the desired property as described above.

Proof.

Denote
R\triangleq([\mathtt{write}]\mathbin{;}(<_{s_{i}}\cap<_{s_{\bar{i}}}^{-1}))
.
Assume for contradiction
R\neq\emptyset
.
We begin by proving properties of this relation:

Claim 71.1:

For every
{\langle{w,r}\rangle}\in R
:

1.

{\mathtt{method}}(w)=\mathtt{write}
,

2.

{\mathtt{method}}(r)=\mathtt{read}
,

3.

w,r\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{i}))
,

4.

r\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{\bar{i}}))
,

5.

w<_{L(e_{i})}r
, and

6.

r<_{s_{\bar{i}}}w
.

{claimproof}

First, from definition of
R
we get that
{\mathtt{method}}(w)=\mathtt{write}
,
w<_{s_{i}}r
, and
r<_{s_{\bar{i}}}w
.

Due to
Observation

70
we have
s_{i}|_{\mathtt{write}}=s_{\bar{i}}|_{\mathtt{write}}
.
This means
w<_{s_{i}}r
and
r<_{s_{\bar{i}}}w
together imply
{\mathtt{method}}(r)=\mathtt{read}
.

Now, since
w,r
are ordered by
s_{1}
we have in particular
r\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{1})\oplus(L(e_{2})|_{\mathtt{write}}))
.
As
{\mathtt{method}}(r)=\mathtt{read}
, it must be the case that
r\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{1}))
.
Since
w,r
are ordered by
s_{2}
we also have
w,r\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{2}))
.
If
i=1
then
w<_{s_{i}}r\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{1}))
implies that
w\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{1}))
.
This means in any case we have
w,r\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{i}))
and
r\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{\bar{i}}))
.
From
w,r\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{i}))
,
w<_{s_{i}}r
, and
L(e_{i})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}s_{i}
we deduce that
w<_{L(e_{i})}r
.

Define a partial order
\preceq
on
R
by
{\langle{w^{\prime},r^{\prime}}\rangle}\preceq{\langle{w,r}\rangle}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{\triangle}}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}}r^{\prime}\leq_{s_{i}}r\land w^{\prime}\leq_{s_{\bar{i}}}w
.
Since
R
is finite and non-empty,
it contains a minimal element
{\langle{{w}_{\min},{r}_{\min}}\rangle}
.
This minimal element has the following useful properties:

Claim 71.2:

There is no write operation
w
such that
{r}_{\min}<_{s_{\bar{i}}}w<_{s_{\bar{i}}}{w}_{\min}
.

{claimproof}

Otherwise, by
Observation

70
we have
s_{i}|_{\mathtt{write}}=s_{\bar{i}}|_{\mathtt{write}}
and thus
w<_{s_{i}}{w}_{\min}
. As
{w}_{\min}<_{s_{i}}{r}_{\min}
we get
w<_{s_{i}}{r}_{\min}
and together with
{r}_{\min}<_{s_{\bar{i}}}w
this implies
{\langle{w,{r}_{\min}}\rangle}\in R
.
Since
w<_{s_{\bar{i}}}{w}_{\min}
we have
{\langle{w,{r}_{\min}}\rangle}\precneqq{\langle{{w}_{\min},{r}_{\min}}\rangle}
,
contradicting minimality.

Claim 71.3:

There is no operation
o
such that
{w}_{\min}\leq_{L(e_{i})}o<_{\mathsf{h}({e_{i}})}{r}_{\min}
.

{claimproof}

Assume otherwise.
From
o<_{\mathsf{h}({e_{i}})}{r}_{\min}
we have by definition that
o
responds before
{r}_{\min}
us invoked in the execution
e_{i}
.
We also have that
{r}_{\min}\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{1}))
,
and so it must be the case that
{r}_{\min}
was already invoked in
e_{1}
.
This means that
o
already responded before that invocation in
e_{1}
.
Therefore, by definition
o<_{\mathsf{h}({e_{1}})}{r}_{\min}
and as
<_{\mathsf{h}({e_{1}})}\subseteq<_{\mathsf{h}({e_{2}})}
we obtain
o<_{\mathsf{h}({e_{2}})}{r}_{\min}
.
This also means
o\in\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e_{1}}))\subseteq\mathsf{completed}(\mathsf{h}({e_{2}}))
and thus

o\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{1}))\cap\mathsf{set}(L(e_{2}))
.
Overall we obtain for any
j\in\{{1,2}\}
that

o<_{\mathsf{h}({e_{j}})}{r}_{\min}
and as
{r}_{\min},o\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{j}))
we have
o<_{L(e_{j})}{r}_{\min}
.
In particular
o<_{L(e_{\bar{i}})}{r}_{\min}
and
as
L(e_{\bar{i}})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}s_{\bar{i}}
we deduce
o<_{s_{\bar{i}}}{r}_{\min}
.
As
{r}_{\min}<_{s_{\bar{i}}}{w}_{\min}
we obtain
o<_{s_{\bar{i}}}{w}_{\min}
.
In particular
o\neq{w}_{\min}
and so from
{w}_{\min}\leq_{L(e_{i})}o
we obtain
{w}_{\min}<_{L(e_{i})}o
.
As
L(e_{i})\preceq_{\mathsf{P}}s_{i}
we obtain
{w}_{\min}<_{s_{i}}o
.
Now, consider two cases:

•

If
{\mathtt{method}}(o)=\mathtt{write}
,
o<_{s_{\bar{i}}}{w}_{\min}
and
{w}_{\min}<_{s_{i}}o
together contradict
s_{i}|_{\mathtt{write}}=s_{\bar{i}}|_{\mathtt{write}}
, which holds due to
Observation

70
.

•

If
{\mathtt{method}}(o)=\mathtt{read}
, we get that
{\langle{{w}_{\min},o}\rangle}\in R
.
As shown above we have
o<_{\mathsf{h}({e_{i}})}{r}_{\min}
and thus
o<_{L(e_{i})}{r}_{\min}
, which implies
o<_{s_{i}}{r}_{\min}
.
Overall
{\langle{{w}_{\min},o}\rangle}\precneqq{\langle{{w}_{\min},{r}_{\min}}\rangle}
,
contradicting minimality.
\claimqedhere

Now, let
S=\mathsf{set}(\mathtt{pref}_{L(e_{i})}({{w}_{\min}}))
and
\bar{S}=\mathsf{set}(L(e_{i}))\setminus(S\cup\{{{r}_{\min}}\})
.
Let
s
be the sequence over
\mathsf{set}(L(e_{i}))
induced by the total relation:

(S\times\{{{r}_{\min}}\})\cup(\{{{r}_{\min}}\}\times\bar{S})\cup(<_{L(e_{i})}\cap(\mathsf{set}(L(e_{i}))\setminus\{{{r}_{\min}}\})^{2})

We proceed to show
s
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

67
.

1.

s\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
:

We use
Lemma

39
.
As
\mathsf{set}(s)=\mathsf{set}(L(e_{i}))

and
L(e_{j})\in\mathsf{Spec}_{\mathsf{Reg}}
for
j\in\{{1,2}\}

it is sufficient to show for all
o\in s|_{\mathtt{read}}
that

\mathtt{pref}_{s}({o})|_{\mathtt{write}}=\mathtt{pref}_{L(e_{j})}({o})|_{\mathtt{write}}
for some
j\in\{{1,2}\}
such that
o\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{j}))
.

Indeed:

•

If
o\neq{r}_{\min}

then taking
j=i
we have that
o\in\mathsf{set}(s)=\mathsf{set}(L(e_{i}))
, and we can show
\mathtt{pref}_{s}({o})|_{\mathtt{write}}=\mathtt{pref}_{L(e_{i})}({o})|_{\mathtt{write}}
.
This is true since for all
w\in\mathsf{set}(s)=\mathsf{set}(L(e_{i}))
such that
{\mathtt{method}}(w)=\mathtt{write}
we have

w,o\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{i}))\setminus\{{{r}_{\min}}\}
and thus

w<_{s}o\iff w^{\prime}<_{L(e_{i})}o
.

•

If
o={r}_{\min}
we take
j=\bar{i}
.
From
Section

G.2
we have that
o={r}_{\min}\in\mathsf{set}(L(e_{\bar{i}}))
.
We show that
